# Changelog

## [Unreleased]

### Fixed

- **A failed label-events read closed the very ticket its own evidence should have protected (Autodev #115).** `LabelHandover#events` answered a GitLab outage with `[]`, logged and forgotten — Autodev #62's rule, broken on the one file that never joined its own guard. On `verdict`'s path `[]` is not "nothing happened": `suspect` had already found the evidence for a handover off the labels the caller fetched for free, and the outage on the *second* read (who moved it) erased that evidence into "no handover" instead of merely declining to act on it — reposing autodev's working label on a ticket a human was holding, and risking delivering over their work (the harm Autodev #98/#102 exist for, reached through another door). The narrow `rescue Gitlab::Error::ResponseError` also missed the wider transport family (`SystemCallError`, `Timeout::Error`, `SocketError`, `OpenSSL::SSL::SSLError`, `EOFError`) Autodev #62's third round already converts everywhere else — measured at ~9% of GitLab reads on the production host. `events` now raises through `GitlabHelpers.answer` for all three of its consumers (`verdict`, `moved_since?`, `todo_reapplied_after?`) uniformly; each caller's own boundary declines the row for the cycle instead. `DormantAudit#audit`'s rescue is widened alongside it, for the same read reached through `stop_on_handover`. `ExternalState#notify_stop` — a write, correctly out of this rule's scope — had the same narrow rescue and is widened to the same transport family, staying non-fatal. `test/api_failure_is_not_a_verdict_test.rb`'s `SCANNED` list now includes `label_handover.rb`, proving the omission the list used to only assert.

## [1.0.0-alpha.53] - 2026-09-03

### Added

- **Every GitLab request autodev makes is now counted, and every transport failure is now logged (Autodev #96).** The instruction into GitLab's intermittency and autodev's own load on it answered three of its four questions without a line of product code — the request-volume estimate (~30-45/cycle) was read off the code, not measured, and the hourly failure correlation could not be decided from four hand-timed samples. `GitlabHelpers.build_gitlab_client` is confirmed to be the single place a URL + token become a `Gitlab::Client` (fourteen call sites, grepped, none calling `Gitlab.client` directly), so its return value is now wrapped in `GitlabRequestCounter` — a transparent proxy that counts every call, read and write, without any of the fourteen call sites changing. The proxy also reassigns itself onto the `Gitlab::PaginatedResponse` the gem hands back (the gem sets `parsed.client = self`, i.e. the raw client), so `auto_paginate`'s pages 2..N are counted too — without that they escaped the count entirely, which the alpha-53 neutral review caught. Counting sits at the client rather than inside `GitlabHelpers.answer` because `answer` only ever wraps reads; eleven of the twelve write methods (`create_merge_request_discussion`, `resolve_merge_request_discussion`, `upload_file`, …) are called directly and would otherwise be invisible to the count. Read/write is classified by a short, explicit rule (write-shaped prefixes plus two named irregular verbs, `job_play`/`job_retry`) checked against every distinct client method this codebase calls today. `GitlabRequestStat` is an hourly counter (one row per hour/kind/endpoint, bumped by upsert); `GitlabTransportFailure` is a row per transport failure — `GitlabHelpers::TRANSPORT_ERRORS`'s own family — with millisecond-precision timestamps and the call site that triggered it, so the manual 24h transport probe never needs repeating. Both are exposed through a new passive `HealthReport` check (`gitlab_requests`), reachable at `/healthz(.json)` and `/admin/health` with no view code to write. See `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-09-03-count-gitlab-requests-design.md` for the full design, why point 2 (autodev's share of the instance's traffic) stays out of scope, and what is deferred to a follow-up ticket (the #82 `diff_refs` design question, the #104 host-side/retry angles).

### Fixed

- **The correction pass read against itself (Autodev #92, #93, #96, #107, #108).** A second neutral review of the lot, this time with the first pass's own commit as its target, found that three of the eight fixes had moved their defect rather than closed it and that four had no test capable of failing — verified by mutation, not by reading.

  **The bound put back for #107 was worse than the gap it filled, and it is gone.** `ReviewOutageBound` counted a signature built from the failure's own message, and git writes `Failed to connect … after 75002 ms` — the timing moves every attempt, so the signature changed every poll and the count never left 1. That is Autodev #99's defect exactly, a guard indexed on something that changes by construction, reintroduced by the fix meant to repair one, and inert against the very cause `CLAUDE.md` records as routine (≈9 % of GitLab reads failing by TCP refusal, in bursts). Keyed on a *stable* message it was worse: five occurrences at a two-minute poll interval is a ten-minute burst of GitLab 502s ending in a give-up that asks the client whether their source branch still exists, which is the harm #107 exists for. An outage is not bounded by occurrences; it is bounded by time, `pipeline_watch_max_days` already does that, and its give-up sentence ("watching for N days without ever being able to conclude") is true of both non-spending outcomes. So the module was removed rather than repaired. What the first review's complaint really named — a missing binary waiting fourteen days — keeps its own answer, `:tool_missing`, which does not wait at all, and the `DormantAudit` half of that fix (no activity row per poll) is untouched.

  **The GitLab request counter over-counted after being fixed for under-counting.** `PaginatedResponse#client_relative_path` reads `@client.endpoint` on every page turn, and once the proxy owned the response every message was recorded — two stats per page for one HTTP request, plus a bogus `endpoint` row in the per-endpoint breakdown. The gem's own configuration accessors are now excluded, derived from `Gitlab::Configuration::VALID_OPTIONS_KEYS` rather than listed, and a new test asserts the tally **equals** the number of requests issued, against the gem's real `Link`-header pagination.

  **The `broken` debounce counted the wrong thing, twice.** It ignored the two window arguments its own comment described, so a `broken` from hours earlier plus one fresh probe read as a streak of two and paged immediately; a run is now a run of *polls*, broken by any gap wider than the probe's TTL. And it counted a run of the same status, so a tool alternating between failing and hanging (`broken, unknown, broken, …`) never reached two of anything and never paged — while #108 exists because two total outages went unseen. It counts consecutive **unhealthy** verdicts now; `unknown` alone still reads `ok` on its own (Autodev #62: a probe that could not observe is not a fault), it simply no longer breaks a run.

  **Narrowing a rescue hid it from the guard that holds it declared.** `infra_recheck_still_ours?` was narrowed off `StandardError` — correctly — behind a constant, and `test/api_failure_is_not_a_verdict_test.rb` recognises a swallow by the class names written on the `rescue` line, so the clause vanished from the scan and its declaration was reported dead. The classes are spelled at the site now, with the reason, which is the "escaped the scan" family Autodev #73 closed three of.

  **Four fixes had no test that could fail, and one of them was the self-exclusion the whole boot fix rests on.** Its test closed its own database handle before looking, so the calling process held nothing and it passed with the exclusion removed. It now holds the database open for the duration — the state a real boot is in — with the precondition asserted through raw `lsof` rather than through the code under test. The other three (the `:tool_missing` branch, reached through the real `command_exists?` rather than stubbed past; the narrowed rescue and its `Redactor` scrub; the retry time's timezone) and the pagination count above are each pinned by a test verified red against the fix removed. The branch asserted four times over and exercised nowhere — an unreadable ticket declines the re-arm rather than taking it — has one too.

  **And the documentation.** `CLAUDE.md`'s architecture section still described the review outcomes as the lot had shipped them, including the per-poll activity key this pass had already deleted — the half a parallel branch reads, so it would have been put back. The launchd `AbandonProcessGroup` inference was rewritten in the topology section as well as retracted in this file, since retracting it in one place and keeping it in the other is not a retraction. And "the container VM holds all three paths on the production host right now", asserted in five places, is false: measured three times over, only puma and the Solid Queue worker hold the database, and the VM that held it the day before holds nothing — a transient holder written as a standing fact. The mechanism that made the boot guard refuse is unaffected: the booting process holding its own database is permanent and sufficient on its own.

- **The alpha-53 lot read against itself, and the boot guard it shipped made autodev unbootable (Autodev #92, #93, #96, #103, #107, #108).** Six branches were written in parallel, each without seeing the other five, then integrated; a neutral review of the merged lot found eight defects, and the first was total.

  **`BootGuard` refused every boot (#92).** `handle` knew two answers — reap a holder of the database file it recognised at `ppid == 1`, or raise `ConfigError` — and there is always a holder it does not recognise. `run_boot_guard` runs after `bootstrap` → `setup_database`, so the **booting process holds the database itself**: reproduced with the real `lsof` and `ps`, which return the caller's own pid under a command (`ruby …/libexec/bin/autodev`) that matches neither pattern. A third party may hold them on top, transiently: `com.apple.Virtualization.VirtualMachine` — the container VM danger-claude runs in — was measured holding all three on 03/09 and holding none of them the day after, so that second reason is weaker than first written, while the first is sufficient on its own. Either alone refused the boot, and `KeepAlive: Crashed` turns a refusal into a loop: the exact "boot loop … worse than the leak it replaces" the class comment claimed to have avoided. Two changes. The calling process is excluded, and an unidentified holder is **named in a warning while the boot proceeds** — the asymmetry decides it, a false refusal being a total outage and a false pass being one ghost process that `Supervisor#run`'s `ensure` now stops recurring; `AUTODEV_BOOT_GUARD_STRICT=1` restores the refusal for an operator investigating by hand. And an abandoned tree is identified by its **process group**, not by `ppid == 1`: the holder on the queue side is `solid-queue-worker`, a *grandchild* whose parent is the fork supervisor, so after a SIGKILL it keeps that parent and the old rule never matched the case the guard exists for — measured on the production tree, where all six processes share one pgid. The test is now "this pgid is not mine and its leader is gone", which also leaves a live sibling supervisor's tree alone. Two smaller faults in the same file: `lsof` exits non-zero as soon as one named path is absent *while still printing the valid pids of the others*, so reading its output only on success made the guard a silent no-op on a database with no WAL sibling; and the operator message claimed the file was held "for writing", which `lsof -t` does not report and the production holder does not do. The six existing tests all injected `holder_finder:`, so nothing exercised the real `lsof` + `ps` at all — three tests now do, against a subprocess holding a real WAL database.

  **The infra recheck took the ticket of whoever had picked the work back up (#93).** `resume_recovered_infra` gained a reclaim in this lot, and its population — `fetch_infra_recheck_candidates` — selects on status, flag, reason, MR and clocks, and on nothing a person did. The sweep the reclaim was modelled on asks `untouched_since_giveup?` before it writes, and states in its own comment that this is *the* protection; the infra pass asked nothing. So a human who took an abandoned ticket back, fixed the CI and watched the pipeline go green would lose the assignment — GitLab Community holds one assignee — to a comment telling them their infrastructure had recovered: a false statement on a client's ticket, which is the harm Autodev #98 exists for. The question is now `Autodev::UntouchedSinceGiveup`, extracted from the sweep so both passes ask the same one rather than two copies free to drift, and a read GitLab could not answer declines the re-arm for the cycle — an unreadable ticket is never permission to take it.

  **The review lost `DormantAudit` on the way out, and the first remedy for its missing bound was worse than the gap (#107).** Moving `:tool_unavailable` and `:clone_failed` off `review_failure_count` was right — neither is a verdict on the merge request — and it left the age bound, `pipeline_watch_max_days`, as the only thing ending such a row. Two things were wrong with that, and only one of them was what it looked like.

  The real one: an **absent `mr-review` binary** is a configuration fact, true on every poll until somebody installs it, and waiting fourteen days on it is absurd. It is now its own outcome, `:tool_missing`, giving up at once under `review_tool_missing` — the answer `MissingReviewSkillError` already gets on the other path. (Defensive today: both configured projects declare a `review_skill`, so the binary path is not taken on the current fleet. It is the branch that would be taken by the next project that does not.)

  The apparent one was a missing *counter*, and the second neutral review measured what one costs. A count keyed on the failure's own message is **inert** against the cause it was written for: git says `Failed to connect … after 75002 ms` and the timing moves every attempt, so the signature changes every poll and the count never leaves 1 — Autodev #99's defect exactly, a guard indexed on something that changes by construction, reintroduced by the fix meant to repair one. Keyed on a *stable* message it is worse than inert: five occurrences at a two-minute poll interval is a ten-minute burst of GitLab 502s ending in a give-up that asks the client whether their source branch still exists, which is the harm #107 was opened for. Bounding an outage by occurrences is the wrong instrument. The age bound is the right one, it was already there, and its give-up sentence — "watching for N days without ever being able to conclude" — is true of both outcomes. So the counter was removed rather than repaired, and what remains of this ticket is the give-up for the missing binary and the fix below.

  **The `DormantAudit` half, which is the one that mattered.** The lot wrote an `ActivityEvent` on **every poll** of an outage, and `Issue.without_activity_since` is the clause common to all three of `DormantAudit`'s arms — so for the whole length of an outage these rows left the safety net Autodev #103 had just widened to catch exactly this kind of stranding. `Reviewer#launch_review`, five methods above, gives that as its reason for *not* rescuing `ConfigError` and resuming. The two causes are distinguished in the log instead, which is `WatchBound#log_bound_withheld`'s precedent for a per-poll line during an outage.

  **The GitLab request counter did not count the pages it asked for (#96).** The gem builds its `Gitlab::PaginatedResponse` with `parsed.client = self` — the raw client — so `auto_paginate` fetched pages 2..N straight through `Gitlab::Client#get`, outside the proxy: uncounted, and unlogged when they failed by transport. Eight call sites paginate, one of them (`GitlabMembershipSync`'s `all_members`) with no `per_page` at all, so it paginates from the 21st member on. Both terms of `failure_rate_pct` were therefore short, for an indicator whose whole purpose is to replace hand-timed sampling with a real rate. The proxy now reassigns itself onto the response.

  **`/healthz` paged on one observation (#108).** `danger_claude` answering `down` — 503, an external pager — is the right severity for a tool everything depends on, but `broken` is the catch-all for any non-zero exit matching neither the quota nor the auth signature, so one container hiccup would wake somebody. It now warns on the first `broken` and pages on the second consecutive one, while `auth_refused` and `binary_missing` stay immediate. Calibrated the way Autodev #60 calibrated `mr_review`'s thresholds, and free: the nine-hour Docker outage produced 262 in a row.

  **And four smaller ones.** The retry time on the errors tab rendered in UTC while the application already has `activity_time_zone` for exactly that, so "reprise prévue 12:34" meant 14:34 in Paris — the only absolute time on the card, with nothing beside it to be compared against. The reclaim wired into the `--reset` CLI was structurally unreachable (`--reset` filters `status: 'error'`; `needs_attention` is only ever true on `done`), so it was twenty lines of code that would be maintained as if they ran, in a repository that has spent four tickets on guards that turned out to be inert — removed, with the reason written where a future widening will read it. `IssuesController#reclaim_before_reset` rescued `StandardError`, presenting a `NoMethodError` to the operator as "Réinitialisation refusée : undefined method …" and refusing the gesture, which is the reverse of the rule `GitlabHelpers::TRANSPORT_ERRORS` states — it now names the failures a reclaim can have and scrubs the message through `Redactor`. And two tests asserting that the automatic revivals reclaim nothing asserted only the resulting status, so they passed whether a reclaim had been attempted or not; they carry a tripwire now.

  **Three claims in this changelog were corrected rather than defended.** The `AbandonProcessGroup` sentence attributed to launchd a distinction between a deliberate stop and a crash-restart that Apple does not document and that nothing here tests — the plist and the shared pgid *were* measured, that inference was not, and it was load-bearing for the `ppid == 1` filter the review replaced. "Twelve call sites" of `build_gitlab_client` is fourteen. "Five call sites" of `safe_mark_failed!` is nine, as the ticket's own instruction said.

- **A dying supervisor now takes its children with it (Autodev #92).** `bin/autodev` spawns `bin/rails server` and `bin/jobs start` in the *same* process group — the ticket's own hypothesis, that a new process group left them unreachable, does not hold; production's `ps -o pid,ppid,pgid` shows one group for the whole tree. The real gap was that `Supervisor#run` had no `ensure` around `shutdown_children`, so any abrupt end of the method — an exception out of `spawn_all` after the first child, an exception out of `wait_loop`, a signal the trap does not cover — left every already-spawned child running. `KeepAlive: Crashed` in the LaunchAgent plist made that permanent: launchd restarts the supervisor on a crash without stopping the process group, so the orphaned puma (and, whenever the death landed after both spawns, the Solid Queue supervisor and its three forks) got reparented to pid 1 and kept the SQLite file, its WAL and its shared-memory file open for writing. Measured on the production log: 39 of the last 63 boots ended with a `rails-server` spawn and no further supervisor line at all — one more ghost process and ~60 MB every time.

  `run` now wraps `spawn_all` + `wait_loop` in an `ensure` that calls `shutdown_children` — idempotent already (`send_term` skips a child that is not `alive?`, `force_kill_stragglers` rescues `ESRCH`/`ECHILD`), so running it on every exit path, including a partial `spawn_all`, is harmless and tears down exactly the children that exist.

  A `SIGKILL` leaves nothing to run, so no in-process fix can reach a child that is already orphaned when a *new* supervisor boots — it belongs to a different, already-dead process by then. `Autodev::BootGuard` (`lib/autodev/boot_guard.rb`) runs once before `bin/autodev` spawns anything, and the alpha-53 neutral review (G1) rewrote both halves of what it does, because the first version made autodev **unbootable**.

  It identifies an abandoned child of ours by its **process group**, not by `ppid == 1`. The holder of the database on the queue side is `solid-queue-worker`, a *grandchild* whose parent is the fork supervisor: after a SIGKILL the fork supervisor is reparented to pid 1 but the worker keeps its parent, so `ppid == 1` never matched the very process the guard exists for. The whole tree shares one pgid (measured on the production host), an orphaned tree keeps the dead supervisor's, so the test is "this pgid is not mine and its leader is gone" — which also leaves a live sibling supervisor's tree alone.

  And a holder it cannot identify **warns instead of refusing**. There is always one: this runs after `bootstrap` → `setup_database`, so the booting process holds the database itself (reproduced: `lsof -t` returns the caller's own pid, whose command matches neither pattern), and a third party may hold them on top: `com.apple.Virtualization.VirtualMachine` — the container VM danger-claude runs in — was measured holding all three on 03/09 and holding none of them the day after, so that second reason is transient where the first is permanent and sufficient on its own. Either alone refused every boot, in a loop under `KeepAlive: Crashed`. The asymmetry decides it: a false refusal is a total outage, a false pass is one ghost process that the `ensure` above now prevents from recurring. `AUTODEV_BOOT_GUARD_STRICT=1` restores the refusal for an operator investigating by hand. The calling process is excluded from the holder list, and `lsof`'s output is read even when it exits non-zero — it does that as soon as one named path is absent, while still printing the valid pids of the others, which had made the guard a silent no-op on a database with no WAL sibling.

  The supervisor's own lifecycle log (`AppLogger#print_console`) was block-buffered — `$stdout.puts` with no `sync`, so a boot that died right after logging one line could lose it, which is exactly what left those 39 boots with no account of themselves at all. `AppLogger.new` now sets `$stdout.sync = true`.

  `AbandonProcessGroup` needed no change, and what is claimed for it is now confined to what was measured (alpha-53 neutral review, G7a). Measured on the production host: the plist carries `KeepAlive/Crashed = true` and no `AbandonProcessGroup` key, and the whole tree shares one process group id. **Not** measured, and therefore no longer asserted: that launchd kills the group on a deliberate stop but not on a crash-restart. Apple documents the key as acting "when a job dies", without that distinction, so the ghost's survival is an observation (39 of the last 63 boots, one `puma` orphan seen on 01/09) whose mechanism is not established. Nothing in the fix depends on the distinction any more: `Supervisor#run`'s `ensure` covers every abrupt end this process can react to, and `BootGuard` now identifies an abandoned tree by its process group having lost its leader rather than by `ppid == 1`, which was the one place the unverified reading was load-bearing.

- **The probe that already exercises `danger-claude` now keeps every verdict it reaches, not only one (Autodev #108).** No new probe was added: `Autodev::UsageGate.probe!` was already calling `danger-claude` once per poll cycle to check the Claude quota, and that call proves the whole chain — binary on PATH, container runtime reachable, volumes resolved, credentials accepted, quota available. But `UsageChecker#available?` collapsed every outcome into one boolean, true unless the output matched the rate-limit signature. Two total outages two days apart went unseen this way: a dead credential (`api error: 401`, 02/09 ~16:16) read `available: true` because the quota was *also* exhausted at the same moment and that signature matched first; a nine-hour Docker engine outage (API 1.55/1.54 mismatch, every call failing in `ensure_volume` before a container even started) answered `available: true` 262 times in a row, and the `/healthz` at the heart of it carried an unrelated `warn`.

  **`UsageChecker#verdict` replaces `#available?`** and answers one of six typed statuses instead of a boolean: `available`, `quota_exhausted` (existing signature, unchanged precedence), `auth_refused` (`AuthFailureDetector::PATTERN`, already written and already used by real `danger-claude` calls, just never run through the probe), `binary_missing` (`Errno::ENOENT`), `broken` (any other non-zero exit — no Docker-specific pattern was written; a non-zero exit is an answer, whatever produced it, and adding a pattern would buy a label, not a detection), and `unknown` (a probe that could not answer at all: a timeout, or any other exception — a read that did not happen accuses nobody). The probe also gained a 30s bounded spawn (six times the observed p50 of a successful run on production, eighty times the whole-container baseline), reproducing `ProcessRunner`'s TERM-then-KILL sequence so a hung probe cannot hang the poll cycle nor leave a container running.

  **`Autodev::UsageGate` records the cause, not only the boolean.** `#available?` keeps answering exactly what its three callers (`IssueProcessJob`, `PipelineMonitor`, `PollDispatcher`) already ask — "may we spend a `danger-claude` call?" — now false on `quota_exhausted`, `auth_refused`, `binary_missing` and `broken` alike, since every one of them means the same call would fail. `unknown` still fails open: a verdict the probe could not reach is a failure to *observe*, not a fault, and the doctrine that a failed observation must never stop the pipeline still holds — only now it is narrower, and correctly so.

  **A new `danger_claude` health card** on `/admin/health` and `/healthz` (also addressable at `/healthz/danger_claude`) reads the same recorded verdict and answers `down` — the same 503-paging tier as `migrations` — on `auth_refused`, `binary_missing` and `broken`, naming the cause and, for `broken`, quoting a Redactor-scrubbed, truncated excerpt of the probe's output. `quota_exhausted` and `unknown` stay `ok` here: an exhausted quota is not a fault of the tool, and it already has its own alarm. The pre-existing `claude_usage` card is narrowed to that alarm alone — `warn` on `quota_exhausted` only, not on every other failure it used to fold in.

  **The dashboard banner and the pipeline's quota deferrals now name the cause too**, rather than always reading "Quota Claude épuisé" / "Claude usage exhausted" — a dead Docker engine used to produce that exact sentence, which is the family of defect this whole ticket exists to end. The three fault causes get their own banner wording (`danger-claude est en panne` / `danger-claude is broken`) saying a human has to act; `quota_exhausted`, and any row recorded before this change (no `status` in its payload), keep the original wording verbatim.

  Related, not implemented here: Autodev #107 (an infra outage spending the review budget) and Autodev #103 (a 401 parking a request where no pass looks for it) are both consequences of the same two incidents this ticket makes visible.

- **A row that errors with no retry scheduled sat in `error` where no pass would ever look at it again (Autodev #103).** `PollDispatcher#fetch_retryable` requires `next_retry_at IS NOT NULL AND next_retry_at <= now`; `DormantAudit#error_arm` only asked "is the budget spent" (`retry_count > max_retries`). A row that errors on its first attempt — budget unspent, but no retry scheduled at all — satisfies neither. `handle_auth_failure` (a dead Claude credential) is deliberate about that: retrying against dead credentials cannot produce anything. What was missing was the exit. Measured 02/09/2026: three requests re-armed by the arrears sweep hit a 401 and parked; two (11541, 16424) sat frozen until reset by hand, and a third (15888) came back only by luck, off an unrelated stamp left over from a defect in May.

  **The neighbourhood was wider than the 401.** Two of the three generic error handlers — `PipelineMonitor::ErrorHandler#handle_failure_error` and `MrFixer::ErrorHandler#handle_fix_error` — never stamped `next_retry_at` either, so any pipeline-evaluation or MR-fix error parked its row the same way. Measured over the product's whole life (`transition` rows are never deleted, Autodev #57): 221 activity events across 42 distinct requests took the two generic handlers or `handle_process_error`'s over-budget branch, 17 across 11 requests hit `handle_auth_failure`, and 21 requests stayed parked over a week, one for 79 days.

  **`DormantAudit`'s error arm is now the derived complement of `fetch_retryable`** (`app/services/autodev/dormant_audit.rb`, `app/services/autodev/poll_dispatcher.rb`): a row is dormant when `next_retry_at IS NULL OR retry_count > max_retries`. A row with a *future* stamp inside its budget is excluded on purpose — it is waiting, not dormant — which is the one case a bare negation of "retryable now" would get wrong. `PollDispatcher.retryable?` is now a named class method both sides read, so the two populations are tested against one rule instead of two hand-written predicates free to drift apart again. The arm also gained the activity-age guard `pending_arm` already carries, so a row is not audited inside the same cycle that just parked it. Once audited, a parked row is revived exactly like any other dormant `error` row — reaffirmed budget, no GitLab call spent on a dead credential, `dormant_exhausted` after `dormant_audit_max` fruitless rounds.

  **The mirror defect: a stamp nobody clears.** 15888 came back on 02/09 by luck, off a `next_retry_at` of `2026-05-14` — a residue from an unrelated error in May that survived every transition since, because no handler on the `error` path wrote that column unless it had a reason to. An expired stamp does not mean "retry once": `next_retry_at <= now` reads true forever, so the row was picked up on *every* cycle with no backoff at all. `safe_mark_failed!` (`lib/autodev/danger_claude_runner.rb`) now takes the stamp decision as a mandatory argument — no default, so a new caller cannot repeat this by omission. A caller scheduling a retry passes the moment; a caller scheduling none (the three `handle_auth_failure`, and the two generic handlers, which keep scheduling no retry — whether they *should* retry at all is a policy question this ticket's measurement raised and deliberately left open, since the dormant audit now recovers either shape safely) passes `nil`, which *clears* the column instead of leaving whatever was there. Every one of the nine call sites across `PipelineMonitor`, `MrFixer` and `IssueProcessor`'s error handlers now states its decision explicitly.

  **The errors tab distinguished nothing.** `/errors` no longer exists — its concerns are the `/issues?tab={errors,waiting,delivered_review}` card tabs — so a parked row was already on screen, rendered identically to one that would retry itself in four minutes. `Web::Views::Concerns::WatchCards` now reads the same rule as the audit's error arm and marks a stranded row distinctly from one with a scheduled retry, in both `fr` and `en`.

  **`CLAUDE.md`** corrected in the same pass: the "Row dormant" line of the error catalogue now describes the widened arm, and the Web UI section no longer documents the removed `GET /errors` → `ErrorsController#index` route — believing that route still existed is what made this ticket's second half ("nothing tells you which requests are waiting") only half right.

- **An infrastructure outage no longer spends a review budget, and can no longer abandon a request over it (Autodev #107).** `review_failure_count` used to be, in effect, a counter of "reviews that could not be produced" collapsed into one boolean: the two rescues that fed it covered a clone that never completed, a tool that could not run at all (`danger_claude_prompt` crashing, `mr-review` absent/timed out/crashed/exited non-zero), and a review contract that was absent or off-schema — three unrelated causes, only the last of which is evidence about the merge request under review. Measured: bobette's Docker engine was down from 02/09 23:00 UTC to 03/09 08:26, every `danger-claude` call failing in `ensure_volume` on an API-version mismatch before any container started. powerpanne/core#16030 crossed that window, burned its five review failures inside it — each about two minutes apart — and was abandoned at 03:11 under `review_failures_exhausted`: a GitLab comment, `Development::StandBy`, the ticket handed back to a named person under a broken promise. Its merge request was `mergeable, conflicts no`; nothing was wrong with it.

  `dispatch_review_outcome` (`lib/autodev/pipeline_monitor/reviewer.rb`) now dispatches on three named outcomes instead of `true`/`false`/`:inconclusive`: `:unusable_output` (the contract file is absent or off-schema) is the one cause left that spends `review_failure_count`, unchanged from before; `:tool_unavailable` and `:clone_failed` join `:inconclusive` on the non-spending side, exactly its `resume_watch` mechanism — neither counter moves, the watch clock is preserved, and the row is bounded by `pipeline_watch_max_days` instead. The binary (`mr-review`) path no longer spends the budget on any outcome but success at all: the binary posts its own findings to GitLab, so its exit status carries no verdict autodev can read. Each non-spending outcome writes its own DB-only activity key (`activity_review_tool_unavailable` / `activity_review_clone_failed`, `log_activity_warn` — no GitLab comment, the same precedent `:inconclusive` already set) so an operator reading the issue timeline can tell them apart without opening a log.

  A clone failure moving to the non-spending side reverses an Autodev #74 fix-round-1 choice to count it: it *can* be request-specific (a deleted source branch), but arrives with no evidence to tell that apart from routine GitLab flakiness (Autodev #96 measured ~9% of GitLab reads failing from bobette by TCP refusal, in bursts). Over-spending the budget on an unreadable cause abandons a healthy request; under-spending only costs polls, bounded at `pipeline_watch_max_days`.

  `Issue.reset_for_retry!(reset_budget: true)` — the dashboard's Reset button and the `--reset` CLI — now also clears `review_failure_count`, joining `retry_count`. Before this fix a request abandoned at `REVIEW_FAILURE_THRESHOLD` still carried that count after a reset and could give itself up again on the very next stumble, with no way for the operator who clicked to know that. The automatic revivals (`recover_on_startup!`, `dispatch_dormant_audit`) do not pass `reset_budget:` and keep the budget they did not decide to clear.

  Depends on Autodev #108 for the occasions this closes *ahead of* the reviewer (a broken `danger-claude` now gates the whole cycle before a first review is attempted); this fix covers what is left independent of that gate — a fault starting after the cycle's probe, clone failures, and the reset gesture.

- **Putting a request back to work did not put autodev back on the ticket, so the next cycle closed it with a false "unassigned" comment (Autodev #93/#106).** Since Autodev #60 every give-up hands the ticket back to a human (`IssueAbandonment#abandon_issue` → `hand_ticket_back`) — deliberate and right. Two gestures resumed such a request without reclaiming the assignment: `PollRouter#resume_recovered_infra` (the infrastructure recheck, `dispatch_infra_recheck`) and `Issue.reset_for_retry!` (the dashboard's *Réinitialiser* button and the `--reset` CLI). Both left the ticket assigned to whoever the abandon gave it to. At the next cycle `dispatch_unassignment` found an active row autodev was not assigned to and closed it, posting "autodev : j'ai ete desassigne de ce ticket" — false, since nobody had unassigned autodev. Observed in production 03/09/2026 on powerpanne/core#16030: an abandon at 03:11, a dashboard reset at ~10:20, a false closure at 10:40.

  **The reclaim is now the shared model `ReviewArrearsSweep#reclaim` already exercised in production (Autodev #88, #98), extracted to `Autodev::TicketReclaim`.** Write the assignment, read it back (GitLab Community — one assignee per issue — accepts and silently ignores a write it cannot honour), record the displaced person in `issues.displaced_assignee_id` so the eventual handback goes to them rather than to the author, and announce the takeover by name. `PollRouter#resume_recovered_infra` now reclaims before re-entering `checking_pipeline`, with its own notice (`reclaim_infra_recovered`) naming the recovered infrastructure. A new `Autodev::ResetReclaim` covers the operator's gesture (`reclaim_operator_reset`) — invoked by `IssuesController#reset` and the `--reset` CLI, deliberately **not** by `Issue.reset_for_retry!` itself, which is also called by `revive_stalled!` and `recover_on_startup!` — automatic recoveries of a row that was never handed back and must reclaim nothing.

  **Ordering follows the sweep's own reason.** The working label is reposed first (no `clear_scope:` — that stays the sweep's alone, since only it has asked `untouched_since_giveup?` before writing), then the assignment with its read-back, then the transition. If the assignment cannot be landed, the label is put back and nothing is transitioned — a half-applied resume must not be this remedy's own failure mode. Where no GitLab client can be built at all, the reset is refused outright rather than half-applied.

  **The sentence that hid the gap is corrected in the same pass.** `poll_router.rb`'s comment on `resume_never_reviewed` used to say the infra pass "does not need" the reclaim; every `stagnation_pipeline` row reached `done` through `abandon_issue`, so it needed it as much as the sweep does — it just never got it. `unassigned_stop` (and its activity-log sibling `activity_unassigned_stop`) no longer claims a human unassigned autodev — "je ne suis plus assigne a ce ticket" / "I am no longer assigned to this ticket" is true and sufficient.

## [1.0.0-alpha.52] - 2026-09-02

### Fixed

- **A stop decided by a human now holds against work already in flight (Autodev #97).** Observed in production on 01/09/2026: a request in `reviewing` was closed from the dashboard — `reviewing` → `closed`, verified in the database — and eight minutes later a `reviewing` → `checking_pipeline` transition arrived, written by the danger-claude call that was still running. It had been decided on the in-memory copy of the row, which still said `reviewing`, and `save!` wrote every dirty attribute over the top. The request resumed as if nothing had happened: green pipeline, seventeen discussions, twenty more minutes of work. The close had not closed anything.

  **A lost update, and Autodev #61's shape moved from the entry of a job to its exit.** #61 guards the *dispatch*: a job is dropped when the row no longer holds the status its pass selected on. It says nothing about the *end* of a job, and a danger-claude call runs ten to sixty minutes — long enough for the row to change hands several times.

  **The guard is in `persist_status_change!` and nowhere else.** That callback is the one save every AASM transition goes through, so the rule is written once and covers the review, the discussion fix, the pipeline fix and anything added later; guarding each long-running exit instead would be a list somebody has to keep complete, and the reason this defect exists is that nobody thought to write its first entry. It compares the row's `status` against `aasm.from_state` and raises `StaleTransitionError`. It covers the three human gestures without naming any of them — the dashboard's close and forced transition go through AASM, `reset` through `Issue.reset_for_retry!`'s `update_all`, and the startup recoveries write `status` the same way — because what it compares is the database, not how the database got there. **It is not atomic and does not claim to be**: read and save are two statements, so a row moved in the microseconds between them is still overwritten. The window it closes is the one that existed; making it atomic means optimistic locking on every write path, `update_all` included, which is a much larger change than the defect asks for.

  **Raising rather than skipping the save, because "write nothing" has to mean the whole sequence.** The caller is mid-method and goes on to post comments and move labels. `StaleTransitionBound` carries the one-line answer, wired into `PipelineMonitor`, `MrFixer` and `IssueProcessor` the way `MissingBaseBound` and `InvalidRequestBound` already are, and dispatched from each boundary's existing per-error branch — the shape `AuthenticationError` has there. It matters most for the two that write: `handle_fix_error` and `handle_process_error` answer an unexpected error by marking the row failed, storing the message and posting a GitLab comment, which on a ticket a human has just taken back is autodev arguing with them.

  **What happens to the work already done is decided rather than left to fall out.** Whatever reached the branch stays on the branch — it is on GitLab, nothing local takes it back, and the next poll reads it as it is if the request is ever re-armed. What is dropped is the *claim*: the state change and the comment announcing it, which are the only parts decided against a state that no longer exists.

- **Autodev modelled a GitLab Premium instance, and ours is Community (Autodev #98).** Two properties of GitLab the product held for true do not exist on `source.modulotech.fr`, which answers `enterprise: false` on `GET /api/v4/version` (19.2.4). Both were written down in comments, both justified a guard, and both guards held nothing.

  **Scoped-label exclusivity does not happen.** `LabelManager#manage_labels` sends the whole label list, minus the four *configured* ones (`labels_todo` + `label_doing` + `label_done` + `label_attention`). A workflow label autodev does not own — `Development::Awaiting CR`, which five production rows carry — travels straight back in beside the `Development::Doing` being posed, and the ticket shows two values of one scope. Measured on powerpanne/core#16224 on 02/09/2026, and independently of autodev on #11339, which carries `Awaiting CR` beside `Awaiting Feature Review` with nobody's help. The alpha-50 entry below states the opposite ("what drops it is GitLab keeping a single value per scoped-label key"); it was wrong when it shipped. **A test froze the fiction against a stub that implemented it** — `test_an_unconfigured_end_label_is_dropped_by_gitlabs_scope_exclusivity`, on the exact mechanism the removal depended on. The stub now keeps the list as it is sent, which is what GitLab does, and the removal is autodev's own: `LabelHandover#scope_residue` is the *removal* side of `foreign_scoped`, the definition that already answered "in my scope but not mine" for the *detection* side, reused rather than restated so the `label_attention` and derivation decisions above it cannot drift. It fires only where a caller asks for it and only on the labels autodev owns; the review entry below says why, and corrects the reasoning printed here when this shipped. `ReviewArrearsSweep#repose_working_label` verifies the residue is gone as well as the working label posed, because a ticket showing two states is precisely what makes `LabelHandover#suspect` answer `workflow_moved` on the row the sweep just re-armed.

  **Multiple assignees do not exist either, and that one closed a client's request.** `ReviewArrearsSweep#reclaim` computes `row.assignees | [autodev]` and writes it — autodev *added* to the assignees, never substituted for them, which the alpha-50 neutral review introduced to stop a human being taken off a ticket silently. Multiple assignees are Premium: `assignee_ids: [human, autodev]` is accepted (200, no exception) and only the first survives, with no system note at all when that first one was already there. The comment's own observation that "no production row is co-assigned today" was not a coincidence to note — no row **can** be — so the correction had never corrected anything. Measured on powerpanne/core#16224, 02/09/2026: labels written at 00:30:37 and 00:30:38, transition fired at 00:30:38.859, **no assignment event anywhere**, and at 00:32:03 `dispatch_unassignment` closed the row and posted on a client's ticket that a human had unassigned autodev. He had not — word for word the harm that method's comment says it prevents.

  **The read-back was applied to one write out of two.** Immediately below `reclaim`, the label write *is* read back, citing Autodev #79 ("autodev never resolves a discussion it has not verified") applied to a label, and explaining that an exception is not what a failed label write looks like. The assignment — the one write GitLab Community swallows in silence — was written and assumed. It is read back now, and a re-arm that cannot put autodev on the ticket raises `AssignmentNotLanded`, is reported `incomplete`, and leaves the row `done` with the human still on it rather than transitioning something the next cycle would close. What this deliberately does **not** do is choose the policy: substituting autodev for the human is defensible and so is never entering a ticket a human holds, and that decision belongs with whoever lifts the ownership filter.

  **How the arrears are unblocked, since taking is the only thing Community allows.** `INCLUDE_AUTHOR_HANDBACK=1` — the documented widening that exists to rescue 22 of the 23 arrears — was never doing what it claimed: every row it accepted was transitioned unassigned and closed at the next cycle. `reclaim` now **takes** the ticket, and three things are what separate that from the silent removal the union was introduced to stop. It only happens on a row an explicit flag accepted, and the report names whose ticket would be taken before anything is written. It is **said**, on the ticket, naming the person and promising the handback — what made the old substitution wrong was that it happened "with no comment and no trace anywhere autodev writes", not that it happened. And it is **given back to them**: `issues.displaced_assignee_id` (new column, NULL everywhere else) records who, and `IssueNotifier#handback_target` prefers it over the author — a different person on 4 of the 20 rows and, on one of them, a deactivated account, so the ticket would otherwise have come to rest on nobody. `reassign_to_author` is renamed `hand_ticket_back` accordingly: a method that sometimes hands the ticket to somebody other than the author must not be named for the author.

  **And the protection those tiers rest on had a hole, which widening the filter is what makes dangerous.** "Nobody has touched this row since the give-up" was asked of the ticket only — its comments and its workflow label. Reviewing the **merge request** is the gesture a reviewer actually makes, and it was invisible: the sweep would take the ticket from the very person who had picked the work back up, and post a notice announcing it. `HumanActivity` (`lib/autodev/human_activity.rb`, extracted from `GitlabHelpers` for module length the way `LabelManager` was from `DangerClaudeRunner`) now owns both halves of the question, under one rule — a system note is GitLab talking and a note carrying autodev's signature is autodev talking, which matters because every merge request of this population carries several of the second kind. Measured on powerpanne/core MR 10946: an unresolved human review thread from 28/07, four days *before* the 11/08 give-up, so the filter answered correctly there — by the accident of two dates, not by anything in the code.

  **A third tier of the ownership filter, because the second does not reach the population.** `INCLUDE_HUMAN_HELD=1` accepts a ticket held by any one person, not only by its author — 4 of the 20 rows are held by somebody else, so the tier above declines them however untouched they are. What survives every tier is the check that actually protects a person: nobody has commented since the give-up and nobody has moved the workflow label since. Someone genuinely working the ticket fails both, which is a better guard than a list of usernames and needs no maintenance. An unassigned ticket is in no tier at all — `dispatch_new_issues` filters on assignment, so a ticket nobody holds is one nobody is waiting on.

- **The guard that ends a non-converging fix loop was only reachable from the rounds that converged (Autodev #99).** `run_fix_cycle` ends two ways — `return finalize_no_commits(issue) unless new_commits?(...)`, or push and `finalize_success` — and `discussion_stagnated?`, which both counts the occurrence *and* takes the decision, lived only in the second. So a round that produced no commit, which is exactly what a stagnation looks like from the inside, returned before the guard and did neither.

  **Measured, on a client's merge request.** Powerpanne 15205, 02/09/2026: eighteen rounds over sixteen hours, every discussion answering `discussion_unchanged`, the Claude quota exhausted **twice in twelve hours** — and `stagnation_signatures` reading `count: 1` at round 17. The guard existed, was configured (`stagnation_threshold` 5), was tested, and had counted once. It stopped because it was stopped by hand.

  **The diagnosis in the ticket was wrong, which is worth recording.** #99 was opened supposing the signature was volatile — that autodev's own replies mutated the thread set and restarted the count. The signature is `discussions.map { |d| d[:id] }.sort`, i.e. already the unresolved thread ids and already stable. The defect was a branch that skipped the check, not a computation that answered wrongly.

  **The pipeline twin never had it, and its shape is the correction.** `check_stagnation_and_fix` bails *before* the attempt on the count it already holds, and writes the new one after — the check unconditional, the count gated on a completed attempt, which is what Autodev #71 actually asked for. The discussions side had moved both, and the comment in `FailureHandler` cites it as its own precedent ("that is also the ordering the discussions side has always had"), true of the counting and false of the checking. `finalize_no_commits` now goes through the guard: that round IS a completed attempt — the threads were read, the corrections attempted, danger-claude answered — so counting it is #71's rule rather than an exception to it.

  **And a ceiling above both, because the guard being unreachable is now a thing that has happened.** `MrFixer#fix_round_ceiling` = `stagnation_threshold * 3`, checked at the top of `run_fix_round` before the thread list is fetched, before any clone, before any model call — a row past it spends nothing at all. It is deliberately not a second guard of the same kind: it reads one integer off the row — `discussion_fix_round`, see the review entry below — and compares it, so no future change to the signature or to the branch structure can make it inert. Derived rather than configured, because it is not a policy an operator tunes but the sentence "the guard should have fired long before here". Its own `attention_reason`, `fix_rounds_exhausted`, never `stagnation_discussions`: reaching it means the ordinary bound did not do its job, and a give-up that says "stagnation" would hide exactly the fact worth seeing. `dispatch_infra_recheck` selects `stagnation_pipeline` alone, so the new reason re-arms nothing.

- **A retry resumes the work; it does not deliver it (Autodev #100).** `perform_retry_errored` restored a label chosen on whether the request carried a merge request: with one it posed `label_done`, without one `label_doing`. Both destinations are **working** states — `retry_pipeline!` → `checking_pipeline`, `retry_processing!` → `pending` — so the first announced the work as finished while autodev carried on underneath it.

  **Measured on a client's board.** Powerpanne 15205, 02/09/2026: `error` → `checking_pipeline` at 00:50:03, and at 00:50:04 autodev added `Development::Awaiting Feature Review` and removed `Development::Doing`. That is the PM's review column, and the ticket sat in it for **ten hours** — 00:50 to 11:06 — while rounds 5 to 18 of discussion fixing ran. It is the harm Autodev #63 removed for give-ups (28 tickets on the review board during the 11/08/2026 incident), reappearing on a path #63 did not cover: it corrected the abandon, not the retry.

  **The fork was right when it was written, and a rename moved the ground under it.** "Label-driven workflow with resume from over" declared five labels, and its own commit message defines two of them: `label_mr` "set after MR creation, enables discussion monitoring", `label_done` "set by human reviewer to signal completion". `has_mr ? apply_label_mr : apply_label_doing` was then exactly "restore the label matching where this row is going", and correct. The config was later collapsed to three labels and `label_mr` was **renamed** `label_done` — taking over the meaning that had belonged to a different label — while the call site kept the method name. Nothing about the retry changed; the word underneath it did. So both branches now pose `label_doing` and the fork is gone rather than corrected: there is no question left for it to answer.

  **The other four callers were checked, not assumed.** `finalize_green_done` (green, reviewed, no unresolved thread), `finish_merged_mr` (GitLab says merged), `skip_reentry_already_merged` (a todo label reposed on merged work) and `finalize_question` (the investigation is answered) all end on a delivery. The retry was the only one whose destination was not. `test/retry_resumes_it_does_not_deliver_test.rb` keeps it that way with a derived guard in the shape `test/api_failure_is_not_a_verdict_test.rb` uses — every `apply_label_done` call site in `lib/` and `app/` must be declared with the delivery it makes — and, like that one, it proves each call site is *declared*, never that a declaration is true. Verified by injection: an undeclared caller added to `MrFixer` fails it.

- **The alpha-52 lot reviewed against itself, and six of its own corrections corrected.** #97 through #100 were written one at a time against separate worktrees; read together on the integration branch, four of them reach a state the others created. What follows is what that reading found, in the order the review ranked it.

  **A take that raises after the write left the ticket taken and said nothing (#98).** `reclaim` recorded the assignment by *returning* a value the caller appended to `landed`, so every raise between `edit_issue` and that return — the read-back's timeout, the `displaced_assignee_id` write, the takeover note — lost the fact that a human had already been removed from the ticket. The report line then read `re-arm incomplete` with no assignment mentioned, no handback attempted and no note posted, and the *next default run* accepted the row on `assigned_to_autodev?` alone: word for word the "silent theft" the comments around `reclaim` say the design prevents. The fact is now written where it becomes true — `reclaim` takes `landed` and pushes to it on the line after `edit_issue`, the rule `repose_working_label` already applies. `restore_assignees` clears `displaced_assignee_id` with the same write, so a rolled-back take leaves no record of a displacement that did not survive.

  **The round ceiling counted a loop it was not about (#99).** `fix_round` is incremented by two loops that measure different things — `FixCycle` counts rounds of unresolved threads, `PipelineMonitor::PipelineFixer` counts attempts at failing jobs — and nothing resets it between them. `fix_round_ceiling` is the sentence "the guard that bounds the discussion loop should have fired long before here", so reading the shared counter gave up healthy requests: thirteen pipeline rounds, each on a different failing job so the pipeline's own guard never counted to its threshold, then two discussion rounds, and the ceiling firing with a comment blaming a guard that had counted twice. `issues.discussion_fix_round` (new column, 0 everywhere, deliberately not seeded from `fix_round`) counts the discussion loop alone and is what the ceiling reads. `fix_round` keeps its meaning and its three readers — the log line, the GitLab notification, the dashboard — because those are about the work done on a request, which is the sum of both.

  **`StaleTransitionError` escaping a poll pass took the project's whole cycle down (#97).** The boundary went into the three workers; the poll decides transitions in line, outside any worker. `check_external_state` rescues `Gitlab::Error::ResponseError` alone, so a refusal there reached `PollDispatcher#dispatch`'s `rescue StandardError`, which logs and returns — taking with it the passes of that cycle that had not run yet, for every row of the project. `PollRouter#route` has the same shape with `ApiUnavailableError`, and its own comment already carries the reason the boundary must be per row. Both now name `StaleTransitionError` beside the error they already caught, and both make the same gesture the workers make.

  **And two `MrFixer` transitions sat above the rescue that was supposed to cover them (#97).** #97's clause is in `MrFixer::ErrorHandler#handle_fix_error`, reached from `execute_fix_cycle`. `transition_no_discussions` and the ceiling's `abandon` both fire from `run_fix_round`, above it, and `MrFixer#fix` declares no `rescue StandardError` — so on a row closed while the round ran, the refusal escaped to ActiveJob and the job landed in Solid Queue's failed executions: a human needed, for the one thing `fix`'s own comment says its clauses exist to avoid. The clause moved to `fix` itself, which is the seam every round exits through.

  **Clearing autodev's label scope destroys the only evidence that a human took the ticket back (#98).** `LabelHandover#suspect` reads exactly one thing to answer "somebody moved this on": a value in autodev's scope that is none of the configured four. #98 removed that value on **every** label write. On the retry path — a row in `error`, a human having moved the ticket to `Development::Awaiting CR` without reassigning — `restore_working_label` wiped it before any handover check had run, and since `error` is not in `PollDispatcher::ACTIVE_STATUSES` no check had run: autodev kept working a ticket somebody had claimed, silently, where before #98 the next cycle closed it with a handover comment. The clearing is now **opt-in**, and exactly one caller opts in: `repose_working_label`, whose only caller is the arrears sweep — the one path that asks `untouched_since_giveup?` before it writes. The review recommended instead checking the handover on the retry path; not doing the damage is smaller than checking for it afterwards, and it costs one cycle of latency rather than an immediate refusal, because the row becomes active and the next `dispatch_unassignment` finds the value still there. The structural remedy — reading the handover from resource label events, which no write of autodev's can erase — is a ticket of its own, as is the gap this uncovered: `error` rows are never handover-checked at all.

  **The guard confining that clearing held for neither configured project (#98).** It tested whether the label being applied is *scoped*, on the reasoning that "reposing the unscoped entry label is not a claim over `Development::*`". Both configured projects declare a scoped entry label: ff/fast/core's only one is `Development::ToDo`, in autodev's own scope. So the guard passed exactly where it was written to fail, and a `needs_clarification` parking — autodev waiting on a reviewer — would have wiped that reviewer's column. The predicate is now ownership, not scope: `label_doing`, `label_done`, `label_attention` and nothing else.

  **A last one the review raised without ranking it: the report named the wrong reason.** A row declined because somebody had touched the ticket since the give-up was printed as `assigned to user 42, left untouched`, sending the reader to a question about ownership that was not the one. `ownership_verdict` answers *why* rather than *whether*, each read still paid for exactly once, and `ownership` only puts the verdict into words.

- **The cause written down for Autodev #95 was wrong, and the correction makes the fix stronger.** Shipped in alpha 51, the error catalogue and the technical guide both said a merge request in conflict has no resolvable diff, so no position yields a line code, so the refusal was permanent. Measured the same evening: the same merge request, still `has_conflicts: true`, published seventeen anchored discussions four hours later, once GitLab had computed its `diff_refs` — on alpha 50, without the fallback. The condition was **transient**, which is what Autodev #82 said in August and what this correction had declared mistaken. What a refusal does not carry is whether it will ever succeed, and that is exactly why bounding it is the right remedy rather than diagnosing it: the bound is safe under both readings. Documentation only; the code was never predicated on the permanence.

### Changed

- **Both usage guides synced with the lot.** The technical guide gains what an operator has to be able to look up: the arrears sweep's third ownership tier and the fact that a re-arm now takes the ticket and hands it back, the discussion-fix section's account of a guard that was only reachable from the rounds that converged and of the ceiling above it, the `fix_rounds_exhausted` give-up, the two catalogue rows for a human's stop against work in flight and for what a retry poses, and the reason clearing autodev's label scope is opt-in. The functional guide gains one sentence, in the answer that already told a reader how to take a ticket back: the gesture now holds while a long job is still running, where before a closed request could restart on its own a few minutes later with nothing to say so.


## [1.0.0-alpha.51] - 2026-09-01

### Fixed

- **A request GitLab refuses is not a request GitLab could not answer (Autodev #95 / #62).** `GitlabHelpers.answer` converted **every** `Gitlab::Error::ResponseError` into `ApiUnavailableError`. That is the right reading of a 500 or a timeout — nothing was read, come back next cycle — and the wrong reading of a 400: GitLab answered, promptly and precisely, that the request cannot succeed as it is formed, and re-sending it produces the same answer for ever. Production, 01/09/2026, request powerpanne 15205, five polls in eighty-seven minutes (18h15, 18h35, 18h49, 19h10, 19h42), the same line each time: `Pipeline check for MR !11258 could not conclude: GitLab did not answer the mr_discussion read: Server responded with code 400, message: 400 Bad request - Note {:line_code=>["can't be blank", "must be a valid line code"]}`. The chain: the merge request is **in conflict**, so its diff carries no resolvable line codes; the skill review runs correctly for eighteen minutes and produces its findings; `ReviewPublisher` posts them as **inline** discussions, each with a position; GitLab refuses every position; `answer` calls it an outage; `launch_review` returns the row to `checking_pipeline` with neither counter spent — correctly, for an outage — and re-raises, so the poll aborts at `PipelineMonitor#check` *before* `abandon_expired_watch`. The next cycle pays for the whole review again. Measured cost: close to 90% of a worker thread, continuously, with no bound reachable at all; the line had to be cut by hand to stop the quota being spent. Twelve of the twenty-three requests of the Autodev #88 arrears are in conflict, so this was the precondition for resuming that sweep.

  **The classification.** `GitlabFailure` (`lib/autodev/gitlab_failure.rb`) is where the two readings of a failed call are told apart — `answer` owns the rule (a failed call has no representation as data), that owns the reading — and it is the exact counterpart of what the alpha-50 lot did in the other direction: that round *widened* the family to every way a call fails to complete (`SystemCallError`, `Timeout::Error`, `SocketError`, `OpenSSL::SSL::SSLError`, `EOFError`), this *narrows* the one member that can also mean something else. `INVALID_REQUEST_STATUSES` is **400 and 422 only**, read by status rather than by class because `Gitlab::Error::STATUS_MAPPINGS` names thirteen codes and every other 4xx arrives as a bare `ResponseError`. Every code left out was checked rather than assumed: a **404** is read as a full answer *inside* three blocks (`ReviewSkillSource#file_on_ref?`, `TargetBranch.of_merge_request`, `GitOperations#branch_exists_on_remote?`) and — decisively — is read back **off the converted exception** by `ReviewSkillSource#confirm_ref!`, the one place a 404 becomes `target_branch_missing`, so moving it would have broken Autodev #89's fix; **401/403** are a property of the credential and not of the request, which is what `MrReviewTokenProbe` and the health card exist to say, and retrying is right while a human rotates a token; **429** is a rate limit, where coming back later *is* the answer; **405/406/409** stay outside as the conservative default, since the failure direction of that list is that a code wrongly called "invalid" can end a request. A 5xx is not one line less protected than it was.

  **The class.** `InvalidRequestError` is a **subclass** of `ApiUnavailableError`, for `MissingTargetBranchError`'s reason and not as a convenience: what the parent encodes is "this unit of work cannot conclude, so nothing may be read off it", which is exactly the situation, so `PipelineMonitor#check`, `MrFixer#fix`, `InfraRecheck`, `PollRouter#route` and `IssueProcessor` all keep behaving correctly with no new rescue clause anywhere. A sibling class would instead land in `check`'s generic `rescue StandardError` on one boundary and in **no** rescue on the other — ActiveJob, Solid Queue's failed executions, a human needed for something no cycle retries. What it must not inherit is the **waiting**, so `describe` is overridden and the line an operator reads now names the refusal instead of blaming an outage.

  **The fallback comes first.** `ReviewPublisher` already knew one way GitLab declines a position — it accepts the discussion and hands back a note whose `position` is null — and demoted that finding into the summary comment. The 400 is the *other* way it declines the same thing, and `post_finding` now reads it identically: the finding is published in the **unanchored summary comment**, never dropped. What the demotion does *not* settle is whether the request may then be **delivered** — the first round of this ticket assumed it did, and the neutral-review entry below is where that was corrected. The rescue names `InvalidRequestError` and nothing else, so a 500, a timeout, a reset connection, a 401 and a 404 all still abort the publication — an outage must not be answered by silently downgrading a review that would have posted properly next cycle. `lib/autodev/review_publisher.rb` joins `test/api_failure_is_not_a_verdict_test.rb`'s scanned perimeter and the derogation is declared there with what was audited under it; `InvalidRequestError` and `MissingTargetBranchError` also join the scanner's `NAMED` alternation, without which the one derogation this rule exists to make visible would have been invisible to it.

  **And if the fallback is refused too, the line stops.** `InvalidRequestBound` (`lib/autodev/invalid_request_bound.rb`) is Autodev #91's shape one endpoint over: it counts occurrences of the **same** refusal — `cause_signature` is the endpoint, the status and what GitLab said, so a different endpoint, a different status or a different complaint is a different fact and restarts the count — and past `stagnation_threshold` gives the request up under `gitlab_refused_request`: `abandon` → `done` + `needs_attention` + `label_attention` + the ticket handed back to its author + a GitLab comment naming the call and the status, with the three i18n sinks in fr and en. Wired at both boundaries that can reach it, `PipelineMonitor#check` and `MrFixer#fix`, because `dispatch_pipelines` and `dispatch_discussions` each re-enqueue their whole population every cycle. It is deliberately **not** `review_failures_exhausted`: the review succeeded, and a give-up that says otherwise is the lie Autodev #85 is about. It is terminal — nothing re-arms the row — and that is what makes rescuing this safe at all, since a row handed back to the watch writes an activity line every cycle, which takes it out of `DormantAudit`'s active arm for ever and restamps the age clock (Autodev #74, #81). No `confirmed?` flag is needed, which is the one place this differs from #91: that error was raised both by evidence and by an outage wearing the same exception, whereas an `InvalidRequestError` is only ever built from GitLab's own 400/422 and is evidence by construction. **The bound's own cost is stated, because the defect's was**: when the fallback itself is refused, the give-up takes five polls and each of them pays for a complete skill review again — about ninety minutes of model time on the production profile, one further round of the defect, spent once, against a loop that had no end at all.

  **The neighbouring verdict was instructed and left alone.** `:inconclusive` — the review that could not be published because GitLab had not computed `diff_refs` yet — spends nothing by design and looked like it had no bound either. It has one: it ends the poll **normally**, so `abandon_expired_watch` (the last statement of `poll_open_mr`) is reached, and `restore_watch_clock` has put back the `checking_pipeline_since` the poll started with, so the age the bound reads is the true one. It is capped at `pipeline_watch_max_days` and signalled as `pipeline_watch_expired`, and that is now pinned by a test rather than argued. What the refusal did *not* have is exactly that: it left by exception, and the exception skipped the bound. The difference is the whole ticket.

- **A review whose findings could not be anchored is not a delivery (Autodev #95, neutral review).** Five findings from an independent review of the fix above, the gravest of which the fix itself created. **(1)** When *every* finding was demoted, `publish` answered a Hash, `publish_from_contract` read that as `true`, `review_count` moved to 1, and the next poll asked GitLab for the unresolved threads of a merge request that carries none — a summary comment is a note, not a resolvable thread — so `no_discussions` was true and the request ended `done`, unflagged, under `label_done` (`Development::Awaiting Feature Review` on powerpanne), on a merge request in conflict whose `changes_requested` verdict and `error` findings existed only as prose. Measured, not supposed: `STATUS=done attention=false reason=nil label_done=true`, and twelve of the twenty-three requests of the #88 arrears are in conflict, i.e. this was the new terminal state of the very population the ticket serves. The cause is more general than the conflict that revealed it — **`contract.verdict` was read nowhere** outside the summary comment's heading, so the verdict held the delivery only by accident, through the threads the findings happened to be anchored on. It holds it itself now: `SkillReviewer#unanchored_verdict?` weighs `changes_requested` against `posted`, and a review that anchored nothing gives the request up under **`review_findings_unanchored`** — `abandon` → `done` + `needs_attention` + `label_attention` + handback + comment, the shape of #63, #81, #85 and #91, and never a reason that blames the review, which ran and judged. `approve` is deliberately untouched: it asks nobody for anything, and `publish` reports `already_published:` as a fact rather than leaving it to be inferred from two zeroes — a cycle that died after posting the review left its anchored threads on the merge request, and the delivery gate is the right reader of them. **(2)** Nothing on the give-up path reads `has_conflicts` / `merge_status` / `detailed_merge_status` (`ReviewArrearsSweep` does; these two boundaries do not), so the three `gitlab_refused_request` texts supplied a cause autodev had not established — "the commonest cause is a merge request in conflict" — on a client's ticket, under a diagnosis any refused endpoint reaches, a refused `mr_discussions` read included. They now quote **what GitLab actually answered** (`answer:`, scrubbed and truncated) and say so; the `/errors` explanation likewise stops claiming that the summary comment was necessarily attempted, which was only ever true of the publication path. Adding the read instead was weighed and refused: it would put a GitLab call on the give-up path at the one moment GitLab is known to be refusing calls. **(3)** "%{count} times running" was not running. `ConsecutiveOccurrences` restarts a count when the *signature* changes and on nothing else, so a healthy cycle between two refusals neither counts nor clears; the behaviour is kept — it is #91's, both facts are deterministic, and clearing on a good cycle would let an alternating refusal cost a full review for ever — and the **sentences** are what changed, in both bounds and in both languages ("with the same answer", "for this branch"), with the module's header and a new `test/consecutive_occurrences_test.rb` stating exactly which sequence is counted. **(4)** `gitlab_failure.rb` and `consecutive_occurrences.rb` — the classification point of the whole family and the counter behind both bounds — were outside `test/api_failure_is_not_a_verdict_test.rb`'s `SCANNED`, the third time in a week a guard's perimeter did not follow new code. Proved by mutation before and after: a `rescue StandardError` in `GitlabFailure.refusal_status` and in `ConsecutiveOccurrences.bump` left the guard green at 17 runs / 0 failures, and now names both files. **(5)** Two inaccurate statements in a lot that documents two others: `review_publisher.rb` said `GitlabHelpers::INVALID_REQUEST_STATUSES` (the constant is `GitlabFailure`'s) and `an_invalid_request_is_not_an_outage_test.rb` said `IssueProcessor#branch_exists_on_remote?` where `gitlab_failure.rb` correctly says `GitOperations#`.

### Changed

- **One definition of "how many occurrences of the same fact" (Autodev #95).** `ConsecutiveOccurrences` (`lib/autodev/consecutive_occurrences.rb`) holds the `issues.stagnation_signatures` bump that `MissingBaseBound` wrote out for Autodev #91 and that `InvalidRequestBound` needed a second time. Both are boundaries mixed into `PipelineMonitor` *and* `MrFixer` and both bump on the identical rule, so writing it twice is how the two drift. What is shared is the counting and never the decision — the same division `MrState` and `TargetBranch` draw: each bound keeps its own key, its own threshold, its own log lines and its own give-up reason. The sequence it counts is one of **occurrences, not of polls** — a cycle in which the fact was not true records nothing, so it neither counts nor clears — which the neutral review of this ticket made the module's header, both bounds' sinks and `test/consecutive_occurrences_test.rb` say out loud.
- **One definition of "what HTTP status did GitLab answer" (Autodev #95).** `GitlabFailure.response_status` replaces the private copy `Autodev::MrReviewTokenProbe` carried. Both ask the same question of the same exception against different lists — 400/422 for "the request is invalid", 401/403 for "the credential is dead" — and the two lists are now visibly complementary rather than accidentally so.
- **`MrFixer#fix` is the boundary and nothing else.** The round itself moved to `run_fix_round`, the split `SkillReviewer` already draws between `review_with_skill` and `run_skill_review`, and for the same reason: what a reader has to take in at a glance at a boundary is the ways it can end, which are now four.

## [1.0.0-alpha.50] - 2026-09-01

### Added

- **The arrears of the revoked review token: the requests nobody ever reviewed go back to the review (Autodev #88).** 23 PowerPanne requests were given up between the 30/07 and the 14/08/2026 under `review_failures_exhausted`, every one of them with `review_count` still at 0 — the review never ran once, on any of them — and nothing brings them back: the Autodev #75 sweep is written for `needs_clarification`, `dispatch_infra_recheck` selects `stagnation_pipeline` and excludes every other give-up reason on purpose (Autodev #53 — an abandon is not re-armed automatically), and reposing the entry label by hand reaches nobody because `dispatch_new_issues` filters on `assignee_id: <autodev>` while 22 of the 23 tickets were handed back to a human by autodev's own `abandon_issue` → `reassign_to_author`. `bin/rails autodev:recheck_review_arrears` (`Autodev::ReviewArrearsSweep`) is that stock, and only that stock: it selects `done` + `needs_attention` + `review_failures_exhausted` + `review_count: 0` + a merge request, on every project, and sends the survivors back through `PollRouter#resume_never_reviewed` — the AASM event `reenter_to_check_pipeline`, so the `transition` row, the audit log, `stamp_pipeline_watch!` and `persist_status_change!` all run, and the `review_count` of 0 that Autodev #85 stopped overwriting is what makes the next green pipeline actually review the MR. **Four bounds, and each one is a decision that was measured.** *`APPLY=1`*, like the two sweeps before it — a report otherwise, naming every request and its merge request. *`LIMIT`, default 3, ceiling 10*, because 3 is `max_workers` and a batch equal to the thread count never queues behind itself: one re-armed request costs 1 to ~60 danger-claude calls, 1 to 4 CI pipelines and 10 to 30 GitLab notes, and 23 restarting on one cycle is the shape of the 11/08 incident (33 rows unblocked together, 486 comments in two hours). The cap applies only under `APPLY=1` — a report shows the whole arrears — it is taken after every filter on the oldest-first order, an unreadable row spends no slot, and the report says how many eligible requests are left outside the batch, because the manual re-run *is* the spacing: a human back in the loop at every turn, which is what Autodev #53 asks of re-arming an abandon. *An ownership filter that is strict by default* — `assigned_to_autodev?` verbatim, which accepts exactly one of the 23 today and reports the other 22 with their assignee — and widens to "assigned to its author and to nobody else, with no human comment and no workflow label moved by somebody else since `finished_at`" only behind `INCLUDE_AUTHOR_HANDBACK=1`, because the permissive gesture must always be a written one. *And the merge request's real state, re-read now* through `MrState.transient?`, whose fifth reader this is: `opened` is the only candidate, `merged` and `closed` are named and left untouched (`attention_reason` included — antedating an Autodev #66 verdict onto an Autodev #63 abandon would falsify the audit trail), a transient state waits and spends nothing, an unknown one is for a human.
