Its `state`, `detailed_merge_status` and `has_conflicts` are **reported and never filtered on**, and the conflicts are a decision rather than an omission (Matthieu, 01/09/2026): 12 of the 23 merge requests are in conflict, the first row a default run touches is one of them, and resolving a conflict is part of autodev's work — `RepoRebaser#rebase_branch_on_target` rebases onto the target before every write action, hands the conflicts to danger-claude and force-pushes with a lease, for any merge request on any project. Which also means, stated rather than left to be found out: the first request re-armed is the one most likely to reach that force-push, at the correction round after the review, and `LIMIT` is what bounds how many can be in flight. `detailed_merge_status: "checking"` is read as "GitLab has not finished computing", so `has_conflicts: false` is not a fact there. Two mechanical obligations travel with a re-arm and both are verified: autodev is put back on the ticket (without it `dispatch_unassignment` sweeps `checking_pipeline`, finds it unassigned and closes the row at the next cycle, with a comment on a human's ticket), and the ticket is put back on `label_doing`, without which `stop_on_handover` reads a handover and closes the row. The label goes on **first, and is read back**: `manage_labels` answers a GitLab error with a log line and `[]`, so a transient 500 is silent by construction, and the end label it failed to replace is not always one autodev asks to remove — `other_workflow_labels` knows nothing of `Development::Awaiting CR`, which five production rows carry, and what drops it is GitLab keeping a single value per scoped-label key on the write that poses `Doing` beside it. `manage_labels` still skips the write that would change nothing, so a ticket already on `Doing` emits no label event. It is **not a recurring pass**, and the class header says why: a pass that wrote one activity row per cycle on the rows it declines would keep `Issue.without_activity_since` permanently non-empty and the dormant audit could never select them again, and one that re-fired the transition each cycle would restamp `checking_pipeline_since` forever and stand down the `pipeline_watch_max_days` bound (Autodev #74, recalled by #81). So a declined row gets a line on the report stream and nothing else — no activity entry, no GitLab note, no label edit — and a re-armed one gets exactly two writes, the `transition` row and the activity entry the reentry already posts. Idempotent twice over: a re-armed row leaves the population by changing status, and a row **this sweep** re-armed once and that was given up again is recognised from the `origin` its own `transition` row carries (never purged — `transition` is not a machinery kind) and declined, so the sweep cannot loop on it every other run. The origin is what the marker is: the bare presence of a `reenter_to_check_pipeline` event says only "this row went through a reentry once", and that event has three writers.

- **A health card on the credential `mr-review` runs with, armed by the fleet rather than by the clock (Autodev #80).** `Autodev::MrReviewTokenProbe` asks GitLab once per poll cycle whether the token the `mr-review` binary authenticates with is still accepted — one request, `GET /user`, the same call `IssueNotifier#assign_to_self` already makes — and records the verdict as an `ActivityEvent` the new `mr_review_token` health card reads passively, exactly as `check_claude_usage` reads `UsageGate`'s. Two properties are the whole design. **It is armed by the population**: the probe filters first on "does any project still review through the binary" — `bin/autodev`'s own boot-warning predicate, now shared rather than restated — and returns before it calls GitLab, opens a file or writes a row when nothing does. Both configured projects have declared a `review_skill` since 25/08, so today it costs exactly nothing and the card is green; it arms itself on the first project onboarded without one, which is the moment the fault below comes back. A probe that watched a credential nothing uses would produce a card nobody acts on — the inverse of the rule `HealthReport` states at `ACTIVE_STUCK_STATES`. **And a read that failed accuses nobody**: there are three verdicts, not two. `alive`; `revoked` on 401/403 and on nothing else; `unknown` for a 500, a timeout, an absent or unparseable `~/.mr-review/config.yml`, or a verdict older than its TTL (two poll intervals, floor ten minutes) — Autodev #62's rule applied to a probe. Only `revoked` raises the card, at `warn` and never `down`, the same tier as `mr_review` and `review_skill`: a review that cannot run does not stop delivery, so `/healthz` keeps answering 200 while the body carries the warn for a secondary alert. The rows are machinery (`KINDS` + `MACHINERY_KINDS`): invisible in the timeline and the SSE feed, dropped past the retention window. What they carry is the **name** of the configuration key the credential came from — never its value.
- **`mr_review_token`, an optional global config key (Autodev #80).** The GitLab credential handed to the `mr-review` binary. Unset — the default — mr-review shares autodev's own `gitlab_token`. Set, the two are kept apart on purpose, and the separation is then a decision written in autodev's configuration instead of a file left behind in another tool's directory. Validated at startup like every other global: absent is fine, present-and-blank is refused (it would read as the fallback while looking like a separation). Documented in `Config::TEMPLATE` and in the technical usage guide.

### Fixed

- **The review-arrears sweep no longer discards 30% of the arrears, and a re-arm it cannot finish says so (Autodev #88, from an adversarial review of the alpha-50 lot).** Six findings, all of them on `Autodev::ReviewArrearsSweep`, and the first was found twice independently, with a production measurement each time. **(1) The idempotence marker excluded 7 of the 23 requests the sweep exists to rescue, for ever.** `swept_before?` — the first filter applied — looked for *any* `transition` row carrying `event: "reenter_to_check_pipeline"`, with no bound on time or origin. That event has three writers: a human reposing the todo label, `PollRouter#resume_recovered_infra`, and this sweep. Measured on the 01/09/2026 production copy, 7 of the 23 rows already carry one, every one of them dated June or July 2026 — months *before* the August `finished_at` being corrected — and all 7 have an open merge request targeting `master`, so all 7 were eligible. The comment justifying the guard ("in this population the row's own journey to `done` never went through `reenter_to_check_pipeline`") was simply false on production, and the report presented the loss as a safety decision ("re-armed once already and given up again"). Nothing distinguished it from a true skip: a false *positive* on an idempotence marker is invisible, where the `finished_at` comparison the design rejected had only a visible false negative. It was reachable because of Autodev #85: before it, `resume_recovered_infra` wrote `review_count: 1` and took those rows out of the population. The marker is now the **origin of the transition**, recorded by `Issue#emit_activity_event!` when the caller names one (`PollRouter::REVIEW_ARREARS_ORIGIN`, one definition, read by both sides) — chosen over a column of its own and over a second activity row because it is written by the very write it attests to: the marker exists if and only if the move happened, with no second write to fail on its own, and `transition` rows are never purged. A reentry nobody attributed carries no origin, so a human's gesture and the infra recheck are not mistaken for this sweep's. **(2) A failed label write was swallowed and the row was closed with a comment blaming a human.** `rearm` transitioned the row and only *then* reached `apply_label_doing`, whose `LabelManager#manage_labels` answers `Gitlab::Error::ResponseError` with a log line and `[]`. So a transient 500 on `edit_issue` left `checking_pipeline` + `needs_attention: false` in the database with the end label (`Awaiting Feature Review`, `StandBy`, `Awaiting CR`) untouched on GitLab — and `dispatch_unassignment`, which runs *before* `dispatch_pipelines`, read that as a `workflow_moved` handover, closed the row and posted a comment on a client's ticket accusing a human of a move nobody made; the marker of finding (1) then made the repair impossible. The label is now the **first** write of a re-arm and the fact is **read back** (the Autodev #79 rule applied to a label): unless the ticket carries `label_doing` afterwards, nothing is transitioned. That verification also covers a label autodev never asks to remove — `other_workflow_labels` is `labels_todo + label_doing + label_done + label_attention` and knows nothing of `Development::Awaiting CR`, which five production rows carry; what drops it is GitLab's one-value-per-scope rule on the write that poses `Doing` beside it, so it is GitLab's behaviour and not autodev's request, which is exactly why it is checked rather than assumed. **(3) The batch slot was spent before the writes, and a write failure was reported as a read failure.** `rearm` decremented the budget and then made two writes, and any error travelled up to `examine`'s `rescue StandardError` and was counted `unreadable` — whose message says "left untouched; a new run will pick it up". Both halves were false: the slot was spent and the ticket had been written to. `unreadable` (now reported as "could not be examined", since a project missing from the configuration reaches it too) is the absence of a verdict for a row **nothing was written for**, and every read plus the project-configuration lookup now happens before the budget is touched — `router_for` used to run *after* `reclaim`, so an unconfigured project reassigned the ticket and only then raised. A re-arm that starts and stops half-way is a new, separate counter, `incomplete`: it names which writes landed, undoes what it can, and says the row needs a human. **(4) A half-finished re-arm leaked the `INCLUDE_AUTHOR_HANDBACK=1` population into the default one.** A row left `done` but assigned to autodev is swept by nothing (`dispatch_unassignment` ignores `done`, `dispatch_infra_recheck` takes only `stagnation_pipeline`, `DormantAudit` neither), and at the next run `assigned_to_autodev?` answered true — so the widening the flag exists to gate arrived without the flag. The assignment is now the second write, the only one after it is local, and it is handed back to the assignees `examine` read if that last write fails; when the hand-back itself fails, the report says so in those words. **(5) `reclaim` emptied the assignee list.** The strict filter is an `.any?`, so a ticket assigned to autodev *and* to a human passes it — and `assignee_ids: [<autodev>]` took the human off the ticket, silently. Autodev is now **added** to the list, and a write that would change nothing is not made at all (the `manage_labels` rule), which is the one row of the 23 still assigned to autodev. No production row is co-assigned today; the write was wrong anyway. **(6) `LIMIT` had a type check and no ceiling.** Every degenerate value fell on the safe side by luck (absent, `""`, `"abc"`, `"3.7"` → the default 3; `"0"`, `"-1"` → nothing re-armed), but `LIMIT=30` — one keystroke from 3 — re-armed the whole arrears in a single run, which is the 11/08/2026 shape `DEFAULT_LIMIT` cites as its reason to exist. It now declares a range beside its type, the `NumericSettings` doctrine: `LIMIT_SPEC = 1…10` (three times the default, so going faster on purpose stays possible; under half the arrears, so no run can drain them; `max_workers` is 3 anyway). Out of range or not an integer raises `ConfigError` and the task aborts before a row is examined — absent or blank still means the default. **And one finding refuted, on the facts: the conflicted merge requests stay in.** 12 of the 23 are in conflict and the first row a default run touches is one of them; the behaviour does not change, but the justification written in the code did, because it was an evasion — it argued about the review step ("a review reads a diff") when the risk is at the correction round. The real reason is that resolving a conflict is autodev's work: `RepoRebaser#rebase_branch_on_target` rebases onto the target before every write action, hands the conflicts to danger-claude when the rebase stops, and force-pushes with a lease, for any merge request on any project. The implication is now written down rather than left to be discovered: the batch drains oldest-first, so the first request re-armed is the one most likely to reach that force-push on a client branch, and `LIMIT` is what bounds how many are in flight. 20 new cases in `test/services/autodev/review_arrears_sweep_test.rb`, whose GitLab stub now keeps state — a write is visible to the next read, and it models both the whole-list replacement of `labels=` / `assignee_ids:` and GitLab's scoped-label exclusivity — because a verification that reads the ticket back cannot be tested against a stub that answers the same thing for ever.
- **autodev hands `mr-review` its GitLab credential, in the environment and never in argv (Autodev #80).** Nothing did. `ProcessRunner#spawn_process` gives every child `DangerClaudeRunner::CLEAN_ENV`, which only *unsets*; the invocation carried no token in argv either; and autodev reads `GITLAB_API_TOKEN` but never exported it — not from the launchd plist, not from a shell profile. So `mr-review` fell through to its own `~/.mr-review/config.yml`, a world-readable file in another tool's directory last written on 14 April 2026. The token in it was revoked that same month, and **every review that went through the binary failed on `401 Token was revoked` from April to August** — 23 requests of one project abandoned on `review_failures_exhausted` between 30/07 and 14/08, the `mr_review` health card firing on 25 distinct issues on 11/08, and the cause sitting in a log line nobody connected to a credential. Four months of silence. The review step now exports `mr_review_token`, else `gitlab_token`, as `GITLAB_API_TOKEN` — which wins over that file in mr-review's own four-layer resolution, so autodev's configuration becomes the single place a review credential is declared, and therefore the single place a probe can watch it. **In the environment, not in `-t`**: the binary accepts the flag, but argv is readable through `ps` by every account on the machine for the whole run — up to `mr_review_timeout`, an hour by default — and Autodev #10 is this repository's precedent for a PAT leaking that way. The seam is a new `env:` on `run_with_timeout`, merged over `CLEAN_ENV` for one spawn only: `danger-claude` and the `post_completion` commands get nothing, which is pinned in a real child process rather than asserted on a hash. Nothing is exported when autodev declares no credential at all — a blank would override, through mr-review's own precedence, a file credential that works.
- **`/healthz/<check>` stopped answering 404 for four of its ten components (Autodev #80).** The route constraint named the components itself — `poller|workers|queue|claude_usage|issues_error|database` — and had not been updated since; `mr_review`, `review_skill`, `stuck_issues` and `migrations` were unreachable through the per-component endpoint an external probe is meant to poll, with a bare routing 404 and no message. The segment is now only shape-checked, and `MonitoringController#component` validates the name against `HealthReport::CHECKS` — the one list — answering an unknown one with the JSON 404 that says which name was not known.

- **An external tool no longer resolves — nor installs — its gems inside autodev's own bundle (Autodev #77).** autodev is installed by Homebrew in deployment mode with `path: vendor/bundle`, and Bundler does not confine itself to the `BUNDLE_*` variables: it rewrites `GEM_HOME` and `GEM_PATH` **in the process environment** (measured on the production host: `GEM_HOME=<prefix>/libexec/vendor/bundle/ruby/4.0.0`, `GEM_PATH=""`). `DangerClaudeRunner::CLEAN_ENV` named ten variables and neither of those two, so every child of `ProcessRunner#spawn_process` inherited autodev's gem home. The proof is a production trace, not a reading: the `dc_stderr` of one powerpanne review carried a backtrace through `/opt/homebrew/Cellar/autodev/1.0.0-alpha.47/libexec/vendor/bundle/ruby/4.0.0/gems/gitlab-5.1.0/lib/gitlab/request.rb:71` — `mr-review` had loaded the `gitlab` gem out of autodev's bundle. `mr-review` is a single-file `bundler/inline` script whose `gemfile(true)` *installs* what it is missing, so it was also writing its six dependencies into that directory: under a Ruby autodev chose rather than the one on the tool's own PATH, and into a tree `brew upgrade autodev` erases, which makes the next review pay a full reinstall or fail outright. `CLEAN_ENV` now unsets both, alongside the `BUNDLE_*` set and for the same reason — **an external tool must resolve its dependencies as if it had been launched from a terminal**. The fix covers all three spawn sites by construction, because all three read that one hash: `danger-claude` (pure stdlib, so unaffected either way — verified on the installed binary, which requires only `open3`, `optparse` and `tempfile`), `mr-review`, and the `post_completion` command a project configures, which is arbitrary and was equally exposed. Pinned at both levels: `test/process_runner_test.rb` measures the variables **in a real child process**, so it covers both callers of `run_with_timeout` at the helper rather than once per call site, and `test/mr_review_environment_test.rb` walks the reviewer's own path down to `Process.spawn`.
- **`mr-review` is started from a directory autodev chose (Autodev #77).** The call passed `chdir: Dir.pwd` under a comment saying it "keeps the previous behaviour" — the cwd the `Open3.capture3` era happened to inherit, which in production is `/Users/modulotech`, the `WorkingDirectory` of the launchd plist, and from a developer's terminal is wherever `bin/autodev` was typed. It is now `Dir.tmpdir`: neutral, declared, and always present. Nothing in the run depends on the value, which is exactly why it should be stated rather than inherited — `mr-review` clones the MR's source branch itself into `/tmp/mr-review_<mr_iid>_<pid>` and delegates every command with `chdir:` into that clone, and everything else it touches is absolute (`~/.mr-review/mr-review.db`, its tempfiles); all `chdir:` has to be is a directory that exists, since `Process.spawn` fails outright otherwise and that failure would read as a review failure.
- **The correction of the record this ticket was opened on.** Autodev #77 was filed as "`mr-review` reads autodev's `CLAUDE.md` as the project's conventions". That never happened, on any published tag since v0.1.0, and it is written down here so the next reader does not reopen the ticket on the same `grep`: `mr-review` sets `@repo_path = File.join("/tmp", "mr-review_#{mr_iid}_#{Process.pid}")` (`bin/mr-review:522` in 0.4.0), clones into it (`:527-528`), and passes `chdir: repo_path` to every `system` call it makes (`:907` in 0.4.0, `:913` in 0.4.1, the version in production) — so the "repo root" its review prompt means when it asks to "read `CLAUDE.md` at the repo root if it exists" (`:647`) is that clone, and both configured projects do carry a `CLAUDE.md` at their root. The original search missed it twice over: the clone goes through a `git!` helper that prefixes the word `git` itself (`:536-541`), and the directory change is the `chdir:` keyword of `Process.spawn`/`system`, not `Dir.chdir`. Checked on the host too — there is no `CLAUDE.md` at `/tmp`, `/private`, `/`, or `~/.claude`, and autodev's own lives under `<prefix>/libexec`, an ancestor of none of the directories in play. `CLAUDE.md` now states that the binary path clones, in the review section and in the error catalogue, which is where the missing sentence sent the analysis wrong in the first place.
- **A request nobody has ever reviewed does not come back from a reentry as if it had been (Autodev #85).** `ResumeHandler#reenter_via_pipeline_check` wrote `review_count: 1` flat, and the comment above it stated that as a decision: run `green_post_review` rather than `green_first_review` at the next pipeline tick, and dodge the `MAX_REVIEW_ROUNDS` short-circuit. Both are true of the case the line was written for — a request that was *delivered*, whose todo label a human reposes, and which must not be reviewed a second time. That request has necessarily been reviewed already: `finalize_green_done` is reachable through `green_post_review` alone, so its counter is 1 before the line writes 1. A request given up **before** any review carries 0, and 0 is not a placeholder, it is the fact that no review round ever completed. Writing 1 over it makes `PipelineMonitor#green_branch` answer `:post_review` at the first green pipeline after the reentry: the review is skipped, the unresolved-thread list is empty because no review ever opened a thread, and the request ends `done` under `label_done` — `Development::Awaiting Feature Review` on powerpanne. Autodev announces work as reviewed to the very people whose job is to review it, which is exactly the harm Autodev #63 removed from the six abandon paths, reproduced by the seventh way out. The counter is now **capped, not posed** (`reentry_review_count`: `0 → 0`, `1 → 1`, `n ≥ 2 → 1`), so the cap — the only thing the line ever bought — survives while the fact does too. Measured in production: 43 reentries through this line, 11 of them on requests with no review at all, 7 already finished and mislabelled. The seven are left alone; relabelling somebody's board is a human decision, not a migration.
- **And the automatic caller is the bigger half.** Two callers reach that line. The human one — `handle_reenter` → `open_mr_destination` → `:pipeline_check` — needs somebody to repose a label. The other, `PollRouter#resume_recovered_infra`, is fired by `IssueProcessJob#perform_recheck_infra` over `PollDispatcher#fetch_infra_recheck_candidates`, whose population is `needs_attention: stagnation_pipeline` — a request whose CI never recovered, which is overwhelmingly a request that stalled *before* any review ran. Nobody reposes anything there and nobody looks. One fix covers both because there is one line; the comment on `resume_recovered_infra` said "review/fix counters reset" and now says which of the two is capped.
- **The activity line stopped lying with it.** `log_activity(existing, :reenter_to_fix)` renders "Reentree (MR ouverte) → verification des discussions a corriger", which describes `green_post_review`. On a request at 0 the next step is the review itself. A second key, `activity_reenter_to_review`, is chosen from the counter, in both locales, and the log line follows it.
- **The invariant on the same line is now tested.** `review_failure_count: 0` beside it really is a reset and must stay one: without it a request abandoned on five consecutive review failures re-enters at 5/5 and gives itself up on the first stumble. Nothing covered that. `test/reentry_does_not_invent_a_review_test.rb` drives the whole thing through `PollRouter#route` and `PipelineMonitor#check` rather than through `handle_green`, because the defect is in the routing and a test that wrote the columns by hand would survive the routing changing underneath it.
- **The review reads its skill from the branch that decides, and the probe and the review now ask one question (Autodev #89).** The review step and the start-up probe read two different branches, and nothing said so. `SkillReviewer` cloned `issue.branch_name` — the MR's *source* branch, and `git clone --depth 1 --branch <b>` is single-branch, so nothing else was even fetchable — and looked for the declared `review_skill` in that clone; `Autodev::ReviewSkillProbe` (Autodev #81) asked GitLab about the project's `target_branch`. Measured on production on 31/08/2026: **13 of the 23 branches** of the ticket-88 population carry neither `.claude/skills/<skill>/SKILL.md` nor the flat layout, while the probe answered "2 declared, 2 present" — and it was right. So a branch could be reviewed by nothing at all: `MissingReviewSkillError`, rescued by `launch_review`, and the request given up under `review_skill_missing`, a **terminal** abandon no pass re-arms. Request powerpanne 15842 went that way on 28/08 — the reason named the correct immediate cause and invited a false conclusion, since the project does declare a skill and the skill does exist, one branch over. The probe's own comment called the target branch "a faithful proxy" because autodev cuts its branch from it: true at the cut, false from the first commit after it. **The branch that decides is a target branch and never the branch under review** — the source branch is the one a merge request can modify, and the one Autodev #79's ruling excludes. *Which* target it is was settled twice in this lot and the review round of it corrected the second answer: the review holds a merge request, so it asks `TargetBranch`'s question 2 (the branch that merge request is going into), and the probe holds none, so it asks question 1 (the project's `target_branch`, else the repository's default branch). See the review-round entry below. The declared skill is read off that ref over the API (one `get_file` to locate the layout, one `tree`, one `file_contents` per blob — 5 requests per review on powerpanne, 4 on ff/fast/core, a few kB) and written into the clone *before* `SkillsInjector.inject`, so the flat layout keeps being migrated to the canonical one before `skill_available?` looks and a skill autodev injects itself is still written. The whole read is a subtree and not a single file because the skill is multi-file — powerpanne's `mr-review` carries `references/posting.md` + `references/project-checklist.md`, prepare-mr carries `references/divergences.md` — and a lone `SKILL.md` would reference files that are not there; `git show origin/<ref>:…` was not an option for the same single-branch reason the defect came from.
- **The target's copy wins, always, and the branch under review may not supply the rules that judge it.** The declared skill's whole directory is dropped from the clone before the overlay, whatever the verdict — so a stale `references/` file the target branch no longer has cannot be read beside the target's `SKILL.md`, and a branch carrying a skill the target does not have still stops the line instead of judging itself. This is the Autodev #79 ruling ("a resolution is a claim, and it may not be made by the agent that produced the correction") one layer up; an exception for "if the branch has it, use the branch's" would have reinstated the defect for the 13 branches carrying a stale copy.
- **One definition, so the two cannot drift again.** The ref, the layouts asked about and the `SkillsInjector::SKILL_NAMES` derogation now live in `lib/autodev/review_skill_source.rb`, read by both the probe and the review — the shape this repository has already had to remove twice, for `MrState` (Autodev #72) and `SkillsInjector.skill_paths` (Autodev #81), each time after the duplicate had diverged. `test/review_and_probe_read_the_same_thing_test.rb` is the pendant of `test/skill_layouts_are_one_definition_test.rb` for the ref: it derives that neither asker spells `target_branch`, `default_branch`, `skill_paths`, `SKILL_NAMES` or `get_file` itself, and pins the counter-example — over the same GitLab state, a project the probe calls `present` cannot produce `review_skill_missing`.
- **A `target_branch` GitLab does not have is `unknown`, never `missing` — the line waits.** The trap was already live in Autodev #81 and this fix inherits it: GitLab answers `404 Commit Not Found` for a ref that does not exist and `404 File Not Found` for a file that does not, and **both arrive as `Gitlab::Error::NotFound`**, so a deleted or renamed target branch read as a broken configuration and the health card accused the operator of a typo. The ref is now confirmed (`client.commit`) before `missing` may be concluded — on the accusing path only, so the healthy case pays nothing — and the failure to confirm it is an abort, on both readers. What that abort *says* was corrected by the review round: it left as "GitLab did not answer", the one thing that had not happened, and it is now a `MissingTargetBranchError`, bounded and signalled like every other confirmed-missing branch.
- **A failed read is never a verdict here either (Autodev #62 / #67).** Every new read goes through `GitlabHelpers.answer`, so an outage raises `ApiUnavailableError`, which `Reviewer#launch_review` already answers: the row goes back to `checking_pipeline` through `resume_watch` with neither counter touched and the watch clock preserved, then the error is re-raised so the poll aborts at `PipelineMonitor#check`'s boundary. No new recovery branch was needed, and that is the strongest argument for the API route over a second clone. What must never happen — a failed read producing the terminal `MissingReviewSkillError` — is pinned by five scenarios. `clone_and_inject` was split into `clone_for_review` + `inject_skills` + `overlay_review_skill` for this: its `ALLOWED_SWALLOWS` entry in `test/api_failure_is_not_a_verdict_test.rb` claimed "no GitLab read sits under this method at all", which this change makes false, and that file warns in its own text that two such claims had already survived a review with an unprotected `client.issue` underneath them.
- **All three sinks say which branch was looked at.** `notifications.{fr,en}.yml`, `activity.{fr,en}.yml` and `web.{fr,en}.yml` all said "absent du depot sur la branche de la MR" / "on the MR branch". That was the wrong branch *and* a branch nobody should fix. `MissingReviewSkillError` now carries the `ref` beside the skill and the expected path, and the GitLab comment, the activity line and the `/errors` explanation all name it, so the instruction an operator reads points at the branch the skill actually has to be added to.
- **A merge request is rebased onto the branch it targets, not onto the one the configuration names (Autodev #91).** "Which branch does this branch rebase onto" was asked at five places and answered three ways. Four of them wrote `@project_config['target_branch'] || default_branch(work_dir)` out by hand (`RepoRebaser#rebase_branch_on_target`, `MrManager#create_merge_request`, `GitOperations#verify_changes`, `CloneHelpers#build_clone_cmd`); `MrFixer::FixCycle#build_fix_env` answered `default_branch(work_dir)` **alone**, ignoring even the configuration, and that is the value `DiscussionFormatter` computes `git diff origin/<target>..HEAD` from — so a review thread was quoted to danger-claude with a hunk measured against a base that neither the merge request nor the configuration named. The third answer, the target the merge request itself carries, was read by **nobody**: `mr` is in hand in `PipelineMonitor#poll_open_mr` and never travelled to a write action. Two questions had been confused, and only one of them was being asked. "Where do this project's **new** merge requests go" is a property of the project, and the configuration answers it correctly — `create_merge_request` writes that value into the MR, so the two are equal at birth. "Where does **this** merge request go" is a property of that merge request, recorded on GitLab, and they diverge the moment the configuration moves while merge requests are open. Measured on PowerPanne, which moved from `staging` to `master` on 25/08/2026 with merge requests open: on 01/09, 83 open MRs still targeted `staging`, 64 of them carried by an autodev branch, and `master` held 49 commits `staging` did not have. The single live autodev line carrying an MR — #10837, parked in `needs_clarification` — targeted `staging` while the configuration said `master`, so the defect was not dormant. Any write action on such a line fetched `master`, found new commits, rebased the branch onto `origin/master` and **force-pushed** it (a rebase always needs force), while GitLab went on diffing the merge request against `staging`: the MR then displays commits and files the work never touched, and every discussion thread's position is anchored on the previous diff. The rule, now stated once: **a new merge request takes the configuration, an existing one takes the target it carries.** `TargetBranch` (`lib/autodev/target_branch.rb`, registered in `lib/autodev.rb`) is the one definition, on the model of `MrState` (Autodev #72) — `declared`, `for_new_merge_request`, `of_merge_request`, and `resolve` for the two callers that can be in either case. Its `Resolver` mixin carries `target_branch_for(work_dir, mr_iid)`, and `default_branch` moved into it: the repository fallback is what an undeclared `target_branch` *means*, not an answer of its own. What is shared is the answer, not the decision — the rebaser still decides whether to rebase, `verify_changes` whether the implementation produced anything, the formatter which hunk to quote — exactly the division Autodev #72 wrote out for its four readers. `rebase_branch_on_target` now takes a required `base:` keyword, so a caller cannot omit having asked. `verify_changes` measures against `origin/<base>` rather than a bare local branch name, which no longer exists in the clone once the base is the merge request's.
- **A GitLab read that could not answer the target no longer falls back on the configuration (Autodev #67's rule, applied).** The target comes from `client.merge_request`, wrapped in `GitlabHelpers.answer`, so a failed read raises `ApiUnavailableError` instead of quietly producing the configuration's branch — which is the substitution that would have hidden this defect for another six months. Every boundary already handles it: `MrFixer#fix` leaves the row in `fixing_discussions` for `dispatch_discussions`, `PipelineMonitor#check` ends the poll with the row untouched and the stagnation signature unwritten (it is written after `clone_and_fix` returns, Autodev #71), and `IssueProcessor#process` parks the row in `error` with a bounded retry because nothing re-enqueues an active state. Nothing is rebased, nothing is force-pushed, no counter is spent.
- **A target branch that is gone stops the line instead of silently skipping the rebase.** `git log HEAD..origin/<base>`, `git rebase` and the quoted `git diff` all answer *nothing found* for a ref that is not there, which is byte-for-byte what "already up to date" looks like — so a merge request whose target branch had been deleted was not rebased at all, in silence, and the correction ran against a tree measured against nothing. `RepoRebaser#ensure_base_available!` checks `refs/remotes/origin/<base>` after the explicit fetch and raises `MissingTargetBranchError`, a member of the `ApiUnavailableError` family so that every existing boundary treats it as "this unit of work concluded nothing" with no new rescue clause — the answer Autodev #89 settled for the neighbouring case: abort, never an invented verdict. "The line waits" was the half the review round had to take back: waiting is right for an outage and is a leak here, so the wait is now bounded and signalled — see the review-round entry below. The check is local on purpose: it covers every way the base can fail to arrive, and it keeps `Gitlab::Error::NotFound` unambiguous, since the only GitLab read on this path is the merge request's own and a 404 there means the merge request is gone, not its branch.
- **The hunk quoted to danger-claude is computed against the base the branch was rebased on.** `run_fix_cycle` resolves the base once, between the clone and the rebase, and hands the same value to `rebase_branch_on_target` and to `build_fix_env`. They used to disagree by construction, so the correction was made on a tree rebased on one branch while the finding was illustrated with a diff against another — and on a `--depth 1 --branch <x>` clone, where `origin/HEAD` is often unset, `build_fix_env`'s answer was the literal `main` and the thread was quoted no diff at all.
- **No merge request's target is ever rewritten from the code.** Retargeting an open merge request is a product decision, not a consequence of this fix; the 64 PowerPanne MRs were realigned by hand on 01/09. Nothing here writes to `target_branch` on GitLab.
- **The review round of this lot, and what three adversarial readers found in it (Autodev #62 / #67 / #89 / #91).** The six tickets were read again on one root before release. What follows is that round's corrections; every one of them is a defect the lot itself introduced or left standing, and each is pinned by a test that fails on the code as it was.

- **A connection that broke is no longer a review failure (Autodev #62, third round).** `GitlabHelpers.answer` is the single conversion point from "GitLab did not answer" to `ApiUnavailableError`, and it only knew one shape of not answering: `Gitlab::Error::ResponseError`, i.e. GitLab answering with a status. A VPN hiccup answers nothing at all — `Net::ReadTimeout`, `Errno::ECONNRESET`, `OpenSSL::SSL::SSLError`, a DNS failure — and none of those is a `ResponseError`. `SkillReviewer#run_skill_review` sorts its outcomes with `rescue ApiUnavailableError; raise` above `rescue StandardError => e; raise ImplementationError`, under a comment saying in as many words that the split exists so an outage is not read as a review failure: the guard only ever covered HTTP, so **five successive network hiccups produced `review_failures_exhausted`**, `label_attention` and the ticket handed back to its author — the exact defect Autodev #88 is cleaning up after, reproduced by another route inside the same release. And the same cut left `ReviewSkillSource.locate`, called bare at the top of `prepare_review_clone`, parking the row in `reviewing`, which `dispatch_pipelines` does not select: recoverable only by `DormantAudit` two hours later, at the cost of one of its three attempts. `answer` now converts `SystemCallError` (the whole `Errno::*` family), `Timeout::Error` (which `Net::OpenTimeout` and `Net::ReadTimeout` subclass), `SocketError`, `OpenSSL::SSL::SSLError` and `EOFError` as well — exactly as broad as the block, which wraps a GitLab client call and nothing else. What stays outside is stated and tested: `NoMethodError`, `ArgumentError`, `NameError`, `TypeError` and every other programming error keeps travelling as itself, because reading a bug in this repository as "GitLab is down" is the same lie in the other direction.

- **Only a merge request that still carries the work answers for its target (Autodev #91).** `TargetBranch.resolve` dispatched on `if mr_iid`, and `mr_iid` is a column `ResumeHandler#reenter_via_reimplementation` deliberately **keeps** when it re-arms a request whose merge request a human closed without merging. So the next run asked GitLab for the target of a merge request that is over and got a real answer: with the source branch still on the remote, `reuse = true` and the autodev branch was rebased onto the target of a **closed** merge request and force-pushed — the damage this ticket exists to remove, reproduced by its own discriminant, and incoherent with the merge request that run then creates, since `MrManager#find_existing_mr` filters `state: 'opened'`, finds none, and targets the configuration. With the branch deleted, `reuse = false`, nothing fetched that base, and `verify_changes` asked `git log origin/<closed MR's target>..<branch>` on a single-branch clone: git declined, and the caller raised `No changes produced by implementation` on a request whose implementation was never measured — `error`, a retry spent, and a message pointing the reader at danger-claude. `MrState.over?` is now the vocabulary (a sixth reader of `mr.state`, and an allow-list of *endings* where `TRANSIENT_STATES` is an allow-list of *waits* — the asymmetry is deliberate and written down where the lists live), a merge request that is over hands the question back to question 1, and `verify_changes` separates "no commits" from "no such ref" through the one definition, `ensure_base_available!`. Measured on 01/09/2026: PowerPanne's 30 open merge requests all target `master`, which the configuration names, so nothing was live — and four **closed** autodev merge requests target the `staging` the configuration left behind on 25/08, which is the population a reposed todo label walks into.

- **A merge request that no longer exists no longer blocks its request for ever (Autodev #91).** `Gitlab::Error::NotFound` inherits `ResponseError`, so a deleted merge request took the outage route: `ApiUnavailableError` on every poll, indefinitely, on a request nothing will ever be able to read — where the behaviour that predates the ticket (fall back on the configuration) was right. A merge request that is gone carries no work either, so that one case is now answered like a closed one. Every other response failure still refuses to answer: a 500 says nothing about whether the merge request is there.

- **A base the remote confirms it does not have is bounded and signalled, not waited on (Autodev #91 / #67).** Making `MissingTargetBranchError` a member of the `ApiUnavailableError` family was right — every boundary already knows how to end a unit of work that concluded nothing — but it also inherited the *waiting*, and a deleted branch does not come back. That took all four of the pipeline watch's guard-rails down at once: `PipelineMonitor#check` caught the family and logged; `abandon_expired_watch`, the last statement of `poll_open_mr`, was never reached; the stagnation signature, written after `clone_and_fix` returns, was left untouched; and `log_pipeline_poll` collapses with `supersede!` moving `created_at` forward, so `Issue.without_activity_since` kept reading the row as fresh and `DormantAudit` never saw it. Each of those four is correct *for an outage*, so none of them is weakened — instead this stopped being called an outage. `MissingTargetBranchError#confirmed?` is the seam: `git ls-remote` is asked, once, on the path that is about to raise anyway, and only the remote answering that it has no such head (or GitLab describing a merge request that names no target) counts as evidence. "The fetch did not land" and "the remote could not be asked" produce the same exception and keep waiting exactly as before — that is Autodev #67's crest line, and bounding it would hand a healthy request back to its author over a flapping VPN. On evidence, `MissingBaseBound` counts `stagnation_threshold` occurrences of the **same** branch (a different branch is a different fact and restarts the count) and then gives the request up under `target_branch_missing` — Autodev #81's shape: the line stops, and it stops under a reason that names the cause, with the branch in the GitLab comment, the activity line and the `/errors` explanation in both locales. Both boundaries that can reach it are covered, `PipelineMonitor#check` and `MrFixer#fix`, because `dispatch_pipelines` and `dispatch_discussions` each re-enqueue their whole population every cycle and the leak was one full clone per cycle, for ever, with no signal to anybody.

- **The review of a merge request reads the skill of the branch it is going into (Autodev #89 + #91).** The two tickets cut the same case in opposite directions and the visible strings claimed they were one. `ReviewSkillSource.ref_for` routed to `TargetBranch.for_fleet_scan` — question 1, the configuration — justified in the file by "a fleet scan holds no merge request", which is true of one of its two callers: the other is `SkillReviewer#prepare_review_clone`, which is handed an `Issue` and holds `issue.mr_iid`. Autodev #89's own argument does not reach that far either — "a merge request may not supply the rules that judge it" excludes the **source** branch, which nothing there reads, and says nothing about preferring the configuration's target to the merge request's, since a merge request cannot modify its own target. So Autodev #91's rule carries: a merge request is reviewed under the conventions of the branch it will be merged into, whatever the configuration happens to declare today. The probe keeps question 1, correctly — it sweeps every project once per cycle with no merge request in hand — and `test/review_and_probe_read_the_same_thing_test.rb` still holds, because the two answers coincide wherever the configuration and the merge request agree, which is the whole fleet as measured. The strings said the opposite in both languages ("la branche cible du projet, celle qui fait foi pour la revue de %{mr_url}", "that branch decides the review, never the MR's own") and are corrected in `notifications.{fr,en}.yml` and `web.{fr,en}.yml`.

- **And the neighbouring string that accused GitLab of an outage it did not have.** Autodev #91 overrode `describe` on `MissingTargetBranchError` precisely so a branch that is gone would not be reported as GitLab going dark; the neighbouring case in Autodev #89 kept doing exactly that. `ReviewSkillSource.confirm_ref!` let `GitlabHelpers.answer` turn the `404 Commit Not Found` into "GitLab did not answer the review_skill_ref read: …", while GitLab had answered precisely and correctly that the branch is not there. It now raises `MissingTargetBranchError` with `confirmed: true` — the repository is the authority on which refs it carries — so it is bounded and signalled like any other missing base, and every other failure of that read is still an outage.

- **The skill subtree is read whole, and its read is bounded (Autodev #89).** `blobs_under` called `client.tree(..., recursive: true, per_page: 100)` and iterated the result — the only list read in the lot without the `.auto_paginate` this repository pairs with `per_page: 100` everywhere else. GitLab caps `per_page` at 100 and `Gitlab::PaginatedResponse#each` walks the current page only, so the answer was silently truncated at 100 entries; and `materialise` drops the clone's copy of the declared skill **first and unconditionally**, so a skill whose `SKILL.md` did not make page 1 left an empty directory and produced `MissingReviewSkillError` — a *terminal* give-up asserting that a file is absent which `locate` had just found on that very ref, one request earlier. Not reachable on today's fleet (powerpanne's `mr-review` is 3 blobs, `prepare-mr` 2) and the last route by which an incomplete read produced an abandon, which is this lot's whole subject. The corollary from the same reading: the subtree was downloaded one `file_contents` per blob with no ceiling on the request count or the bytes, so `MAX_SKILL_BLOBS` (200) and `MAX_SKILL_BYTES` (5 MB) now bound it, and exceeding either is an `ImplementationError` — what this path already means by "the review could not be run and GitLab is not at fault", bounded by `REVIEW_FAILURE_THRESHOLD` — rather than a terminal claim that a file is missing, or a silent truncation.

- **A read that could not answer whether a branch exists no longer answers that it does not.** `GitOperations#branch_exists_on_remote?` returned `false` for every `Gitlab::Error::ResponseError`, and `false` is a decision branch: `reuse` false sends `resolve_branch` to `create_branch`, which cuts a fresh branch and fetches no base. A 502 on that endpoint therefore threw away a branch that was there and then measured the work against a ref the clone does not have. Only the remote answering `404` may produce `false` now; everything else raises through `GitlabHelpers.answer`, which is the Autodev #62 shape this call site had been left out of.

- **`verify_changes` is pinned on a real repository, with a real merge request (Autodev #91).** The comment above it describes the case where the base is the merge request's target and not the branch the clone checked out — and nothing mounted that case: mutating `origin/#{base}` to a bare `#{base}` left `test/mr_target_branch_is_the_rebase_base_test.rb` entirely green, real git repositories and all, with only a test on a stubbed command string going red. The scenario is now built on disk, single-branch clone included, and the mutation is caught.

- **And the fourth copy, found by integrating (Autodev #89 + #91).** The two tickets were green on their own branches and red together: Autodev #89's `ReviewSkillSource` restated both halves of the answer Autodev #91 had just made single — `project_config['target_branch']`, and its own repository-default fallback. Neither author could see it; the guard `test/target_branch_is_one_definition_test.rb` derives its verdict from the tree, so the merge is where it fired, which is the whole point of writing it that way rather than as a list. The probe's fallback is a GitLab read where the workflow classes' is a `git symbolic-ref` in a clone, because a fleet scan holds no work directory — so `TargetBranch` now carries both mechanisms for the one question (`repository_default`, and `for_fleet_scan` for the case where no merge request exists *and* no clone is in hand), and the probe asks instead of answering. Split across two files they were free to disagree about what an undeclared `target_branch` means.

- **The derived guards of the alpha-50 lot were put through mutation, and three of them could be walked past (review round, Autodev #85 / #89 / #91).** A guard that claims to protect an invariant without doing it is worse than no test at all: it reads as a guarantee. Each tightening below was proved the same way — apply the mutation, watch the guard go red, restore — and the mutation is named so the next reader can repeat it.

- **"Both askers route through the shared module" was satisfied by a comment (`test/review_and_probe_read_the_same_thing_test.rb`, Autodev #89).** Its `code_of` dropped whole comment lines only, so a **trailing** comment stayed in what it counted as code, and the assertion was `include?('ReviewSkillSource')` — a name, not a call. Demonstrated: `Autodev::ReviewSkillProbe` carrying its own copy of `ReviewSkillSource.declared`, with the module named in two tail comments and called nowhere, left all seven tests of the file green. The splitting now lives in `test/ruby_source_helper.rb` (`RubySource`), shared with `SwallowScanner`, which had written it correctly and documented why the order matters. Two answers, named for what they do rather than both called "the code": `uncommented` cuts the trailing comment and **keeps string literals**, for a guard looking for a literal (`config['target_branch']` is the offence, and blanking literals is exactly how it would hide — verified: with literals blanked, a `project['target_branch']` planted in the probe is invisible); `blanked` also blanks literals, for a guard looking for a keyword, because a log message quoting the word "raise" once made a rescue clause look like a re-raise. The assertion is now `/\bReviewSkillSource\.\w+/`, a call. And the perimeter is derived instead of listed: an asker is any file under `app/` or `lib/` whose code mentions the module, so a third one added tomorrow is scanned without anybody remembering it — the two-element list could not see a third asker, and Autodev #89 exists because nobody saw the second one. Two more assertions close the ends: the perimeter cannot silently empty (the two known askers must be in it), and the module has to still be where the layouts and the read are answered, asking `TargetBranch` for the ref.

- **Two sentences in that same file had become conditional, and are now stated and pinned (Autodev #89 + #91).** The round that aligned the review on Autodev #91's rule moved it onto question 2 — the target the *merge request* carries — while the probe stayed on question 1, the target the *configuration* declares. "Over the same GitLab state, the two agree — same ref asked about" and `test_the_probes_verdict_and_the_reviews_outcome_agree_on_every_state` stopped being unconditional properties the moment that landed: they hold wherever the two targets coincide, which is every project of the fleet as measured and what the fixture builds, and a merge request targeting anything else can legitimately give `present` to the probe and `review_skill_missing` to the review. The test is renamed to say the condition, and the divergent case is now a test of its own rather than a paragraph: one repository carrying the skill on `master` only, a merge request going into `release/2027-q1`, the probe answering `present` and the review `missing` — both correct, because they are answers to two different questions. It has teeth: reverting the review to question 1 (`locate` without `mr_iid`) makes it fail.

- **The single-definition guard on the target branch was satisfiable through a public method of the module it protects (`test/target_branch_is_one_definition_test.rb`, Autodev #91).** `CONFIG_READ` and `REPOSITORY_FALLBACK` matched neither `TargetBranch.repository_default(@client, @project_path)`, which is to the letter the third answer the ticket deleted — the repository's value with the configuration ignored, `build_fix_env`'s — nor `default_branch work_dir` without parentheses, nor `client.project(path).default_branch`, nor `config.fetch('target_branch')`, nor `config[BRANCH_KEY]`. Demonstrated: that first substitution in `FixCycle` left all three source tests green. Four changes. The private steps of the module are now **derived from its own surface** — every `module_function` of `TargetBranch` except the three entry points a reader may call (`declared`, `resolve`, `of_merge_request`) — so a new public half-answer is in the perimeter the moment it is written. The repository fallback is matched as the bare identifier, which costs one declared exception and closes three spellings at once. The configuration read covers `.fetch` and `.dig`, and **constant aliases whose value is the key**, read off the tree so a new alias is covered without editing the file. And the perimeter is `app/` **and** `lib/`, which retires the third test's claim to *derive* that `lib/` was enough — it grepped `app/` for three method names, so renaming the action satisfied it; what it now asserts is where the write primitives live (`git push`, `git rebase`, `--force-with-lease`, `create_merge_request`), which cannot be renamed away. Four exceptions are declared by name with the reason, and the one that is a *shape* rather than a purpose is checked instead of believed: `ReviewSkillSource#ref_for` supplies `repository_default` **as `resolve`'s block**, and the guard asserts the call sits inside one.

- **That guard also documented a derogation that no longer exists (Autodev #89 + #91).** It named, as a knowingly-out-of-perimeter hand-written copy, `Autodev::ReviewSkillProbe#ref_for` spelling `project['target_branch']` … `client.project(...).default_branch`. The integration fix removed it — the probe delegates to `ReviewSkillSource`, which asks `TargetBranch` — and the paragraph stayed on the state before. It is the failure mode `ALLOWED_SWALLOWS` documents about itself ("two plainly false declarations survived a review"), reproduced in a file written the same week; every declaration in the rewritten list was re-read against the code it names. Two more sentences there were imprecise: `test_an_existing_merge_request_goes_where_it_says_it_goes` is now `test_a_merge_request_that_still_carries_the_work_goes_where_it_says_it_goes`, with the concluded case as its own test, because "exists" is the weaker half of the rule and not the one the code implements; and `test_the_answer_is_read_off_whatever_shape_gitlab_returned` passes a hash with **no `state`**, which only reaches the target because `MrState.over?` is an allow-list — load-bearing, unstated, and now both stated and pinned by a test of its own.

- **The perimeter of "a failed read is not a verdict" had not followed the lot (`test/api_failure_is_not_a_verdict_test.rb`, Autodev #62's guard).** The file instructs its own reader to "re-read this paragraph before adding a file, and add the file if the reason no longer holds", and none of the five tickets in the lot did, while every one of them moved GitLab reads into files it does not name. Demonstrated: a `rescue StandardError → nil` injected into `ReviewSkillSource#locate` — a failed read turned into "the skill is absent", so into a **terminal** abandon, which is the whole subject of Autodev #89 — left every assertion green. Four files added: `lib/autodev/review_skill_source.rb` (five reads on the path to `review_skill_missing`), `lib/autodev/target_branch.rb` (the one read that decides the base of every rebase and every force-push), `lib/autodev/missing_base_bound.rb` (a new boundary, and the decision that ends a request on a base that is not there — no swallow of a read today, scanned so the next one is not free), and `app/services/autodev/review_arrears_sweep.rb`. Seven declarations, each written against the code: `verdict` is the probe's entry point alone and its substitute is a third value (`unknown`, which is not `missing`); `file_on_ref?` reads a 404 on a *file* as GitLab answering, with the "no such ref" 404 separated one level up by `confirm_ref!`; `confirm_ref!` is a re-class that keeps travelling (`MissingTargetBranchError < ApiUnavailableError`) and adds the `confirmed:` evidence `MissingBaseBound` requires; `of_merge_request` substitutes "no merge request carries this work", not a base. In the arrears sweep the two clauses mean different things and the declarations say which: `examine` is the **read** boundary (`:unreadable` — nothing written, no slot spent, re-examined next run) precisely because the writes are caught one level down by `rearm`'s own clause (`:incomplete` — the slot *is* spent, the row stays `done`, the line says a human has to look). A new assertion, `test_no_declared_swallow_is_dead`, fails on any entry whose swallow is gone from the code, because a derogation nobody can see is dead reads exactly like a live one.

- **The one visible claim of Autodev #85 that no test held (`test/reentry_does_not_invent_a_review_test.rb`).** The CHANGELOG says a reentry picks its second activity key on the review counter, in both languages, and that the log line follows. Nothing asserted it: `grep -rn "reenter_to_review" test/` returned nothing, and putting the pre-#85 behaviour back — `:reenter_to_fix` in every case, "will route to fixing_discussions" — left the whole suite green — 2178 runs, 0 failures, as measured by the review, and this file's own four tests green with it. It was the only one of the ticket's five assertions that did not go red when removed. The file already drives exactly that path, so it now reads the activity key off the `activity_events` row (where `ActivityLogger.payload_for` stores it beside the rendered line) on `review_count: 0` **and** on `review_count: 1`, and asserts the log line names the branch the reentry took. Verified against the mutation: two failures with it (the key on `review_count: 0`, and the log line), none without.

- **The locale files' ASCII convention is now held by something (`test/locales_test.rb`).** Every entry written in this lot follows it and nothing checked a single character — neither `locales_test.rb` nor `i18n_derived_keys_test.rb` looked at encoding — so it held by habit. The perimeter is the two tables autodev **writes onto GitLab**, `notifications` and `activity`, and the reason it stops there is measured rather than assumed: `web.fr.yml` carries accented letters on 264 lines, `devise.fr.yml` on 3, `cli.fr.yml` on 2 and `web.en.yml` carries typographic characters on 20 more, and those are rendered into the dashboard and the terminal, never posted — rewriting some three hundred existing labels is a product decision, not a guard's. Two non-ASCII characters are allowed by name (the em dash that separates a message from its interpolated detail, the arrow that names the step an activity label is moving to); an allow-list rather than "no accented letter", so a non-breaking space or a curly apostrophe pasted into a GitLab comment is caught too. And the sentence about the excluded tables is itself pinned: a second test fails the day one of them stops carrying what the rule forbids, and says to widen the perimeter — the kind of statement this lot produced five stale copies of.
- **One reading of "is a review skill declared", and one fewer method than the lot shipped with (Autodev #89, #91, review follow-up).** The guards round left three findings as product changes rather than making them itself, which was the right call and they are made here. `ReviewSkillSource.declared` strips and blank-checks; `Reviewer#launch_review` spelled `.presence` without the strip, `SkillReviewer` spelled the config read raw in two places, and `MrReviewTokenProbe#relied_upon_by_any?` spelled its complement raw — four spellings of one question, only the shared one trimmed, so a value with surrounding whitespace took the skill path on one side and counted as "no skill declared" on the other. Unreachable today (`Project` validates the column as present-or-nil, never blank) and a live divergence all the same, in the lot whose whole subject is that a question has one answer. All four now ask the module, `review_skill` is named in the parity guard's owned set so a fifth spelling fails there, and that guard's header stops declaring the gap as a known limit. The token is the *config read* rather than the bare word, because the bare word is also a legitimate `ActivityEvent` kind and the scan keeps string literals — it must, the config read being one itself. Proven by mutation: putting the raw read back in `SkillReviewer` turns the guard red. And `TargetBranch.for_fleet_scan` is gone — added by the integration fix, orphaned an hour later when the paths round routed `ref_for` through `resolve`, and exactly the second definition the tightened guard now forces anyone to declare.

## [1.0.0-alpha.49] - 2026-08-28

### Added

- **A correction is verified before its discussion is resolved (Autodev #79).** `MrFixer` ran, per unresolved thread, `danger-claude -p` (the fix), `danger-claude -c` (the commit), then `resolve_merge_request_discussion` — and nothing between the second and the third looked at what had been written. The resolution **is** the statement that the review point is dealt with, and the agent making the statement was the agent that produced the correction. The only thing downstream was the pipeline, which answers a different question ("do the tests pass"), so a fix aimed at the wrong thing, or one that removes the symptom without touching the cause, closed its thread exactly like a good one. Worse, `danger-claude -c` committing *nothing at all* also closed it. The defect predates Autodev #74 but that release makes it bigger: both configured projects now declare a `review_skill`, so the findings are produced by the project's own adversarial pass and there are more of them, each more pointed. Each thread's fix is now measured — `git diff <sha before the thread>..HEAD`, so the diff is that thread's correction and not the round's — and handed with the original finding to one **fresh** `danger-claude -p` that answers a two-field contract (`{"verdict":"addressed|not_addressed","reason":"…"}`) in a file under `/tmp`, the `ReviewContract` idiom of Autodev #74. Only `addressed` resolves the thread. The invariant is stated once and holds without exceptions: **autodev never resolves a discussion it has not verified.** The verifier is deliberately not the fixing session — `run_fix_prompt` resumes one session across a round's threads, and resuming it here would be the same defect one layer down — and carries no `-a mr-fixer`, whose whole instruction is how to correct. It is equally deliberately **not** a re-run of the review: the prompt confines it to "does this diff address this finding", because an adversarial pass produces findings on any code and never converges, which is exactly the asymmetry PowerPanne's own review skill draws when it prescribes verifying each fix after triage and forbids re-running the adversarial pass on the corrected commit.
- **Every way the check fails to say yes leaves the thread open.** This is Autodev #62's direction transposed from a GitLab read to a danger-claude call: the neutral value here is `addressed`, which closes a review thread for good, so a check that could not be performed must not produce it. Three causes, three sentences in the activity log, one consequence — `discussion_unverified` (the pass looked and says the diff misses the point, with the verifier's own reason), `discussion_unchanged` (no diff at all; answered without spending a call), `discussion_unverifiable` (the check could not be performed: `git` declined to answer, danger-claude crashed, or the contract file is absent or off-schema). `RateLimitError` / `AuthenticationError` are not caught: they are not verdicts about this correction either, and they travel to `execute_fix_cycle` like every other `danger_claude_prompt` call site, which parks the row with a retry instead of closing anything. An open thread has a recovery — the next round re-reads it, and `stagnation_threshold` rounds of the same open set hand the ticket to a human through the existing `stagnation_discussions` give-up. A wrongly resolved thread has none. The round's success line counts what was **resolved**, not what was found, and a round that resolved nothing says so under its own key (`discussions_none_resolved`) on both sinks instead of announcing "0 discussion(s) corrigée(s)".
- **`fix_verification_max`, the bound on what that costs.** One verification per thread is one extra `danger-claude -p` per thread, and production rounds carry 1 to 18 unresolved threads (mean 7.6 over the 94 rounds on record). The new setting caps how many corrections **one fix round** verifies — and therefore how many threads it attempts at all, since a thread it cannot verify is a thread it must not resolve. The overflow is not "fixed without being checked", it is not fixed this round: the next round picks it up exactly as it picks up a thread whose correction failed, and the activity log says how many were deferred. Default **10**, from the same production sample: 81% of rounds carry ten threads or fewer, so the cap is invisible to four rounds in five, and worst-case extra spend is 10 calls per round against the 36 an 18-thread round already costs. `0` is the sentinel — the check off, the pre-#79 behaviour — and has to be written down by whoever wants it. Declared in `NumericSettings::SPECS` (`0`…`100`) like every numeric setting since Autodev #58, so a bad value is refused at boot in `config.yml` and under `projects:` rather than read as `.to_i` → 0 → protection silently off; resolved `@project_config[…] || @config[…] || 10`, the shape `infra_recheck_max` and `pipeline_watch_max_days` already use. New `lib/autodev/verification_contract.rb` and `lib/autodev/mr_fixer/fix_verifier.rb`; four new keys in `activity.{fr,en}.yml`. New `test/mr_fix_verification_test.rb`, `test/verification_contract_test.rb`.
- **A health check that names a `review_skill` the project's repository does not carry, before the first request runs into it (Autodev #81, the ticket's option 2).** The ticket priced this at "one clone per project"; it is not. The question is "does this path exist on this ref", GitLab's repository-files endpoint answers it in a single request, and the ref is knowable from outside — autodev cuts its MR branch from the project's `target_branch`, so that branch is a faithful proxy for the revision the review clone will carry. New `Autodev::ReviewSkillProbe` asks it once per declaring project per poll cycle (two requests at the current fleet size, nothing cloned or written to disk) from `AutodevPollJob`, alongside the Claude-quota probe and for the same reason: `HealthReport` is passive by contract, so the cycle runs the live read and the card reads the recorded verdict — the same shape `check_claude_usage` has with `UsageGate`. New `review_skill` check on `/admin/health` + `/healthz`, `warn` rather than `down` because the fault is confined to the misconfigured project, with the project, the expected path and the branch in its `meta`. Three verdicts, and **`unknown` is not `missing`**: an unreachable GitLab is never recorded as a broken configuration, which is Autodev #62's rule applied to a read whose answer accuses the operator of a typo. Two verdicts need no request at all — a name that cannot be a skill directory is `missing` on sight (which is where the ticket's option 3, a shape check on the form, ends up: in the one place where it produces a verdict rather than a second opinion), and a skill in `SkillsInjector::SKILL_NAMES` is `present`, since autodev writes those into every clone itself. Advisory throughout: the probe rescues internally and the call site guards it again, so it can never be what breaks a poll cycle. New machinery `ActivityEvent` kind `review_skill` (invisible to the timeline, the sparkline and `/stream`, dropped past the retention window like the other four). One new key in `web.{fr,en}.yml`.

### Fixed


- **Asking a clarification puts the request back on the entry label, so it stays discoverable — and so the board tells the truth (Autodev #75).** `dispatch_new_issues` finds work by asking GitLab for the issues assigned to autodev **and** carrying a `labels_todo` label. `SpecChecker#post_clarification` left the ticket on `label_doing`, so the request left that population the moment the question was asked: reachable in principle, discovered by nobody. It also mislabelled the board — while autodev waits, the work is in the hands of the person who was asked, not in autodev's, and showing it as in progress is what let 12 requests sleep for up to three months without autodev or the PM seeing them. `apply_label_todo` now reposes the entry label the request *arrived with* — `labels_todo` is a list and both of powerpanne's values are in live use by different people (`To do`, `Development::ToDo`, read off the parked tickets' resource label events), so `apply_label_doing` remembers the one it stripped on pickup and that one is put back; the project's first declared value is the fallback for a request that never carried one. `apply_label_doing` strips it again the moment the request restarts, so the ticket never carries two values of the workflow scope, and `LabelHandover` already refuses to read a todo label in the workflow scope as a human handover (twice over: `configured_labels` subtracts `labels_todo`, and `doing_dropped?` will not read a missing `label_doing` as a handover while a todo label explains it).
- **A label edit that would change nothing no longer writes.** `manage_labels` still has to read the ticket, but it skips `edit_issue` when the resulting label set equals the current one. A no-op edit is not free: GitLab records a resource label event for it, and those events are the evidence `LabelHandover` and `PollRouter#reenterable?` read to decide whether a human asked for something new — and the clarification label is re-posed on every poll that finds the request still unanswered.
- **A request waiting for a clarification is reachable again (Autodev #75).** Asking the human a question parked the ticket in `needs_clarification`, and nothing ever re-read the answer: `PollDispatcher#process_issue` is the only code that asks whether a human replied, and the only caller of `process_issue` — `dispatch_new_issues` — runs every discovered issue through `PollRouter#route` first, whose `route_by_state` answered `:next` for any existing row that was not `pending`. The row was dropped one step *before* the question could be asked, on every project that configures the label workflow, which is every real project. `IssueProcessJob::DISPATCHED_FROM[:process]` had accepted `needs_clarification` all along, so the two declarations of "the states a processing cycle may start from" disagreed, silently, and the narrower one won by dropping the row. There is now one declaration, `Issue::PROCESSABLE_STATES`, read by both. Measured on the 18/08/2026 production copy: 12 requests parked on PowerPanne, the oldest since 15/05/2026, every one of them answered on GitLab; the three `clarification_received` transitions in the whole history of the database were all fired by hand from the dashboard's transition button — none carries the `activity_clarification_received` entry the automatic path always writes, and two of them landed 42 seconds apart on two different tickets.
- **The arrears of those 12 requests can be drained: `bin/rails autodev:recheck_clarifications`.** The routing fix only rescues the requests autodev still *sees*, and asking a question does not repose a `labels_todo` label — 9 of the 12 sit on `Development::Doing`, outside `dispatch_new_issues`' population whatever the router answers. The sweep reads the `issues` table instead of GitLab's label filter, re-asks each parked request whether a human answered, and re-queues the ones that did. Reports unless `APPLY=1`; idempotent (a re-armed row leaves the population); a thread GitLab cannot serve is counted `unreadable` and left untouched, never `waiting`. It is the arrears and only the arrears — which pass should own `needs_clarification` from now on moves the ticket on the PM's board either way, so that decision is not taken here.
- **A misconfigured `review_skill` now names itself instead of degrading the project into generic dormancy (Autodev #81, the ticket's option 1).** When a project declared a `review_skill` whose `SKILL.md` was not in the clone, `prepare_review_clone` raised `ConfigError` and `launch_review` let it escape. The refusal to fall back to the `mr-review` binary was and stays correct — that would run a different process from the one the project declared, silently. What the escape cost is everything after it: `pipeline_green!` had already moved the row to `reviewing`, `dispatch_pipelines` selects `checking_pipeline` only, so the row was parked where no poll re-read it. The dormant audit picked it up two hours later by its active arm, gave it three bounded second looks an hour apart, and after five hours at best raised `needs_attention: dormant_exhausted` — "this line stopped moving", never "the declared review skill is not in the repository". The real message existed and was precise, and lived only in the log. Worse, every request of the project took the same road: one typo degraded the whole project into dormancy, one request at a time. The error is now `MissingReviewSkillError`, still a `ConfigError` (so nothing else changes behaviour) but a class `launch_review` can recognise, carrying the skill name and the repository path that was looked for as data rather than as prose in a message. It is rescued and routed to the shared abandon point: `abandon` → `done` + `needs_attention: review_skill_missing` + `label_attention` + the ticket handed back to its author + a GitLab comment naming the skill, the path and what to do about it, plus the activity line and the `/errors` + health-card explanation the abandon point drives. Neither review counter moves — the review never ran, so it neither succeeded nor failed, and spending the five-strikes budget on a configuration fault five more attempts cannot clear would be wrong. **The rescue stops the line rather than resuming it**, which is the whole reason it is safe: Autodev #74 had ruled that rescuing this and handing the row back to `checking_pipeline` writes an activity row on every poll, so `Issue.without_activity_since` never reads the row as dormant, `DormantAudit`'s active arm never sees it again, and each return restamps `checking_pipeline_since` and stands the age bound back up — an unbounded, unsignalled loop, strictly worse than parking. A give-up has neither property because the row never comes back, and the three passes that could re-arm it are each excluded by a different property (`dispatch_pipelines` selects `checking_pipeline`; `dispatch_done_unassigned` excludes flagged rows; `dispatch_infra_recheck` selects `stagnation_pipeline`) — asserted as properties, not as the absence of a symptom. `reviewing` joins the `abandon` event's sources for this: it is the second give-up that starts from there, after `review_giveup`. A **bare** `ConfigError` out of the review still escapes exactly as before, with the pinned test that says why. Three new keys in `notifications.{fr,en}.yml`, `activity.{fr,en}.yml` and `web.{fr,en}.yml`.

### Changed

- **The technical guide's error catalogue catches up with eleven cases the code already handles.** `docs/usage/autodev-technical-usage.md` is rendered at `/admin/help`, and its catalogue had not been extended since the review step forked in two: the four failure classes the skill path introduced (a declared skill missing from the clone, `diff_refs` not yet computed, a GitLab error while `ReviewPublisher` posts, a Claude quota or credential failure *during* a skill review — Autodev #74), the three unreachable-read cases that must abort a poll instead of reading as good news (the jobs endpoint, any read the poll concludes from, the prompt-context read — Autodev #62, #67, #71), the two todo-label-reposed cases the poll router now answers per issue (Autodev #67), the interrupted pre-MR reset that must stamp `next_retry_at`, and the health probe that sees the review broken for everybody (Autodev #60). Also added: `autodev -t TOKEN` to the supervisor's flag table, and the `needs_clarification` row of the technical→métier mapping now carries the exact label `web.fr.yml` renders. The functional guide needed nothing — it was kept in step ticket by ticket. The two configuration-form screenshots (`15-project-edit.png`, `16-project-new.png`) are reshot: they predated `label_attention` (Autodev #63) and `review_skill` (Autodev #74), so the form they showed was missing both fields the release documents.

## [1.0.0-alpha.48] - 2026-08-24

### Added

- **The review step forks on `review_skill`, wiring the last four tasks into the live poll (Autodev #74).** `PipelineMonitor::Reviewer#launch_review` picks the path: a project with no `review_skill` still runs the `mr-review` binary exactly as before; a project that declares one now takes `SkillReviewer#review_with_skill` instead — clone, skill, contract, publish. Both paths answer through the same three-valued dispatch, `dispatch_review_outcome`: `true` → `finalize_review_success` (review count incremented, as before), `false` → `finalize_review_failure` (spends one of the five review-failure budgets, as before), and the new `:inconclusive` — GitLab had not computed the MR's `diff_refs` yet, so nothing could be published — sends the row back to `checking_pipeline` via `review_done!` **without touching either counter**, so the next poll runs the whole review again instead of either delivering the MR with no review posted (counting it a success) or spending a budget on a cycle that never got to act (counting it a failure). `ApiUnavailableError` from a GitLab outage while publishing still propagates out of `launch_review` untouched — it is not a review failure. `finalize_review_success` / `finalize_review_failure` are unchanged.
- **`ReviewContract.parse(json_string)` parses the contract the project's review skill returns, and applies the single rule that decides what becomes an inline discussion (Autodev #74).** A finding is inline when it is **both** anchorable (carries non-blank `file` and numeric `line`) **and** blocking-class (`severity` is `error` or `warning`). Everything else lands in `summary_only`. The rule cannot be applied in the wrong order or in two places: both conditions are checked in a single method, `inline?`, so the classification is consistent across the contract's lifetime.
