Raises `ReviewContract::InvalidError` (subclass of `AutodevError`) on unparseable or off-schema input — malformed JSON, missing verdict, unknown verdict (outside the allow-list `%w[approve changes_requested]`), findings that is not an array, each finding that is not an object, or a severity outside the allow-list `%w[error warning info nitpick]`. Later tasks use this parser to post the findings: the inline set becomes `mr-review`-style discussions, the summary-only set becomes bullets in the MR note. New `lib/autodev/review_contract.rb` with `ReviewContract.parse`, `ReviewContract#verdict`, `ReviewContract#summary`, `ReviewContract#inline`, `ReviewContract#summary_only`, and the exception `ReviewContract::InvalidError`.
- **`review_skill`, an optional per-project setting naming the review step's skill (Autodev #74).** First step of a change letting the review step run the reviewed project's own skill (from the cloned repo's `.claude/skills/`) instead of the `mr-review` binary, where the project ships one. The name has to be declared rather than discovered by convention: the author-side skill is `mr-review` on powerpanne/core but `prepare-mr` on ff/fast/core, where `mr-review` is instead the *reviewer* skill — the wrong role for this step. New nullable `projects.review_skill` column, editable in the dashboard's project config form beside `label_attention`, accepted under `projects:` in `config.yml`, imported by `autodev:migrate_projects_from_yaml`, and emitted by `Project#to_project_config` — later steps read `@project_config['review_skill']` and branch on its presence. Unset keeps the `mr-review` binary, the right answer for a project that ships no review skill. A present-but-blank value is rejected rather than read as "unset", same rule as `label_attention`. `Project::OPTIONAL_LABEL_FIELDS` and `ConfigValidator::OPTIONAL_LABEL_FIELDS` are renamed `OPTIONAL_STRING_FIELDS` now that the "if set, must not be blank" rule covers a field with nothing to do with labels; `ProjectValidator.validate_optional_label_types!` renames to `validate_optional_string_fields!` to match. One new key in `web.{fr,en}.yml`.
- **`label_attention`, an optional fourth per-project label (Autodev #63).** The value a give-up poses instead of `label_done`. It has to be configured rather than derived: the scope autodev computes from `label_doing` + `label_done` names a *scope*, not its values — it is `Development` on both configured projects — and what lives in that scope is project taxonomy autodev cannot pick from (powerpanne/core offers `StandBy`, `Awaiting CR`, `Awaiting Merge`, `NeedEstimation` and five `ToDo <name>` variants; ff/fast/core offers only `NeedEstimation`). New nullable `projects.label_attention` column, editable in the dashboard's project config form beside the other three labels, accepted under `projects:` in `config.yml`, imported by `autodev:migrate_projects_from_yaml`, and emitted by `Project#to_project_config`. **Unset is a defined fallback, not a missing setting:** no end label is written at all and the row keeps `label_doing` — a ticket that looks still in progress is a smaller lie than a ticket that looks reviewed, and it costs no GitLab call. Optional, so it is outside the all-or-nothing label triplet, but not standalone: setting it without the three required labels is rejected, and a present-but-blank value is rejected rather than read as "unset", which would have turned a typo into a silent fallback. Applying `label_doing` or `label_done` now clears it too, so a re-armed row does not end up carrying two values of the same GitLab scope. Two new keys in `web.{fr,en}.yml`.
- **A delivered-but-flagged request now shows *why* the tool gave up (Autodev #59).** Autodev #49 persisted `dc_stdout` / `dc_stderr` on the review give-up precisely so the reason a `mr-review` failed would outlive log rotation, and then nothing rendered them: a `done` row carrying `needs_attention` + `review_failures_exhausted` told the reader that automated review had failed five times and never told them what it said, with the answer sitting in the row's own columns. On the 32 production rows with that reason the answer is usually one line — `Unexpected error: Gitlab::Error::Unauthorized … Token was revoked.` — the difference between "ask the autodev team" and "rotate the PAT". A **Diagnostic technique** card on `GET /issues/:id` now folds each non-empty stream into the same collapsible the /issues watch cards use (`technical-details` / `technical-summary` / `technical-pre`, chevron and all), capped at 320px because a danger-claude capture runs to thousands of characters where an `error_message` runs to hundreds. Scoped to `done` + `needs_attention`, the delivered_review tab's own population: on a live row the two columns hold whatever the last phase left behind, and `needs_attention` alone is not enough because `dormant_exhausted` sets it without finishing the row. Both this card and the pre-existing raw-data card scrub at render, which is what covers the rows written before the capture-path scrub landed — no backfill migration, since the 131 rows in the production copy hold no match for either `Redactor` pattern. Four new keys in `web.{fr,en}.yml`.
- **`ReviewPublisher` posts the parsed review contract to the MR, with autodev's own PAT (Autodev #74).** Third piece of the change letting the review step run the reviewed project's own skill and stop writing to GitLab itself — the skill's PAT (`mr-review`'s) has been answering 401 since April, so a review that reaches the MR at all now goes through the credential autodev already trusts for everything else. `ReviewPublisher.new(client:, project_path:, logger:, locale:).publish(mr_iid:, contract:)` takes a parsed `ReviewContract` and returns `{ posted:, demoted: }`. Three behaviours carry it, each closing a defect this codebase already paid for: (1) GitLab has not always computed the MR's `diff_refs` by the time a poll asks — `publish` returns `nil` in that case, not an empty result, because an empty result reads as a *successful* empty review and would deliver the ticket with no review ever posted (the exact Autodev #62 failure family, by a new route). (2) Every GitLab call goes through `GitlabHelpers.answer`, so a failed read or write raises `ApiUnavailableError` rather than degrading into a value — `test/api_failure_is_not_a_verdict_test.rb` still passes with nothing new to declare. (3) GitLab accepts an inline discussion position it cannot anchor and hands back a note with a null `position`; that finding is demoted into the summary comment instead of being dropped, the same "nothing is silently dropped" rule the reviewed projects' own review skills already carry. The summary comment is posted last and carries a marker (`<!-- autodev:review -->`) so a retried publish (a cycle that died between the discussions and the summary) does not repost the inline discussions a second time. New `review_summary` key in `notifications.{fr,en}.yml`. New `lib/autodev/review_publisher.rb`, required from `lib/autodev.rb`.
- **`PipelineMonitor::SkillReviewer#review_with_skill` clones the MR branch, runs the project's declared review skill, and hands the contract to `ReviewPublisher` (Autodev #74).** Fourth piece of the change letting the review step run the reviewed project's own skill: clones `issue.branch_name` into a scratch work dir with the existing `clone_and_checkout`, injects the standard skills via `SkillsInjector.inject` (the same clone-then-inject idiom `FailureHandler#prepare_work_dir` already uses), then runs `danger-claude -p` with a non-interactive prompt telling the skill to write its findings to a contract file instead of touching GitLab, and parses that file with `ReviewContract.parse` before handing it to `ReviewPublisher`. Returns **three** values, not the usual two: `true` on a completed review, `false` on a review failure (clone/injection failure, a missing declared skill, a `danger-claude` crash, a missing or off-schema contract file), and `:inconclusive` when `ReviewPublisher#publish` returns `nil` because GitLab has not computed the MR's `diff_refs` yet. Collapsing `:inconclusive` into either boolean was the one way to reintroduce the failure family this whole design removes: read as `true` it would increment `review_count` and let the next poll's post-review branch find no discussion and deliver the MR with no review ever posted (the Autodev #62 shape, by a new route); read as `false` it would spend one of the five review-failure-budget attempts on a poll that never got to judge anything. A project declaring a `review_skill` that is missing from the clone raises `ConfigError` naming the skill and the expected path — it never falls back to the `mr-review` binary, which would silently run a different review process under the same "we reviewed this" banner. A clone or skill-injection failure is deliberately a review failure like any other (judgment never started, so there is nothing to retry differently), while a `ReviewPublisher` GitLab hiccup is not (it succeeds untouched next cycle) — made to hold in practice, not just in comment, by normalising both failure sources (`clone_and_checkout`'s `GitError`, a sibling of `ImplementationError` under `AutodevError` rather than a subclass, and any untyped `Errno::*` underneath `SkillsInjector.inject`, which has no rescue of its own) to `ImplementationError` in one narrowly scoped `rescue StandardError`, declared in `test/api_failure_is_not_a_verdict_test.rb`'s `ALLOWED_SWALLOWS` with the reason its substitute is a review failure and not an invented GitLab verdict. `danger_claude_prompt` gained an optional `timeout:` keyword (nil by default, so its five pre-existing callers are unaffected) so the skill run passes `mr_review_timeout` through to `run_with_timeout` instead of running under the ordinary 600s `dc_timeout` fallback — a full skill run's clone, skill load and adversarial pass have `mr-review`'s duration profile, not an implementation call's. New `lib/autodev/pipeline_monitor/skill_reviewer.rb`, included into `PipelineMonitor` next to `Reviewer`. The prompt sent to `danger-claude` needs no i18n — nobody but Claude reads it. A later task wires `review_with_skill` into the review step in place of (or alongside) `Reviewer#launch_review`.
- **A health check that sees mr-review broken for everybody (Autodev #60, item 1).** The missing alert behind Autodev #49. `review_failure_count` is per ticket and `review_failures_exhausted` raises a per-ticket `needs_attention` flag, so when mr-review is down for the whole fleet — expired token, crashing binary, bad release — the product produced N independent per-ticket flags and nothing saying the tool was dead. Nobody correlates three isolated tickets: the real incident ran for weeks and cost three MRs shipped without review. `HealthReport` now carries an `mr_review` check that counts, across all projects, the **distinct issues** that recorded an mr-review failure (activity keys `review_failed` / `review_failures_exhausted`) inside a sliding window, and warns past a threshold — `warn`, not `down`, so `/healthz` keeps answering 200 while the JSON body carries the signal. Both settings are configurable (`monitoring.review_failure_window_seconds`, `monitoring.review_failure_threshold`) and their defaults are **calibrated on the 12/08/2026 production copy**, not guessed: over 4.5 months, the six isolated single-ticket incidents peak at 1 distinct issue in any rolling 6h window (max 6 events), while the 11/08 tool-wide outage reached 25 distinct issues (489 events) in one window. Defaults are therefore 6h / 3 distinct issues — the populations do not overlap, and replaying the outage against them fires at 14:05:28, five minutes after the first failure. Counted in distinct issues rather than events on purpose: Autodev #61's replay bug put 26 identical give-up comments on a single ticket, which an event count would have read as an outage.

### Changed

- **Two claims about the review path now describe what the code does (Autodev #74).** `CLAUDE.md`'s row for a declared-but-missing `review_skill` asserted three things that were false: that the row is left in `checking_pipeline` (it is left in `reviewing`), that the error re-raises every poll indefinitely (nothing re-enqueues a `reviewing` row, so it is raised once and then goes quiet), and by omission that no signal ever reaches a human (`DormantAudit#flag_exhausted!` sets `needs_attention: dormant_exhausted` after three audits, which `/errors` and `/admin/health` both render). And `ReviewPublisher`'s completion-marker comment claimed the marker stops a retry from double-posting the *discussions*: it does not — crash on the second of two discussions, retry, and they come out `["f1", "f1", "f2"]`, because the marker only exists once `post_summary` has run. The spec only ever promised it "keeps a retry from doubling **the comment**", so the behaviour is compliant and the comment was the defect.
- **A give-up no longer poses the "ready for feature review" label (Autodev #63).** Every abandon path called `apply_label_done`, and on the projects concerned `label_done` is `Development::Awaiting Feature Review`: autodev put "ready for feature review" on tickets it had just failed to review five times, given up on after five identical infra failures, or dropped after fourteen days of a frozen pipeline watch. The ticket then reached the PM's board presented as reviewed, which is one of the mechanisms that filled that board during the 11/08/2026 incident — 28 tickets pushed to `Development::Awaiting Feature Review`, three out of four in merge conflict, none of them reviewed. Unlike the Autodev #61 path this one was never silent: it posted a comment and handed the ticket back to its author. What was wrong is the label, and the label is what people read on a board. All **five** give-up paths now pose `label_attention` instead — the four that share `IssueAbandonment#abandon_issue` (`stagnation_pipeline`, `stagnation_discussions`, `pipeline_watch_expired`, `review_limit_reached`) and `give_up_reviewing` / `review_failures_exhausted`, which keeps its own `review_giveup` event but no longer its own end label. What stays per-reason is what Autodev #60 argued should: the GitLab comment and `attention_reason`, since `dispatch_infra_recheck` selects exactly `stagnation_pipeline`. `label_done` remains the label of a nominal delivery, and `LabelHandover` now subtracts `label_attention` from its free-label set — it is a label autodev writes, in the derived scope by design, and a row re-armed by the infra recheck can still be carrying it — while leaving it out of the scope *derivation*, so a project that spells it outside the workflow scope cannot disable the handover rule.

- **One abandon point, through AASM, with one reassignment policy (Autodev #60, item 2).** Four call sites ended a request by writing `status: 'done'` straight to the row — `handle_stagnation` (pipeline), `MrFixer#transition_to_done_stagnation!` (discussions), `give_up_on_watch` (expired pipeline watch) — plus the review-round limit, which fired `pipeline_green!` under a `max_review_rounds_reached?` guard, so a give-up was recorded in the journal as a green completion. Consequences of the direct writes: no `transition` row in `activity_events`, so nothing in the activity journal and nothing in the audit log, and none of the `after_all_transitions` callbacks ran — which is exactly what produced the stale `checking_pipeline_since` clock Autodev #53 had to fix by clearing the column by hand at each site. They also disagreed on assignment: the review path handed the ticket back to its author, the other three left it on autodev, where nobody looks. All four now go through `Issue#abandon` (a new AASM event, legal from `checking_pipeline` and `fixing_discussions`) and `IssueAbandonment#abandon_issue`, which stamps `finished_at`, raises `needs_attention`, applies the end label, reassigns to the author and posts both user-facing messages. (That end label was `label_done` as this shipped and is `label_attention` since Autodev #63, below — the give-up paths are exactly the ones that must not claim a delivery.) `pipeline_green` lost its `max_review_rounds_reached?` transition and `Issue` lost the matching guard flag: `checking_pipeline` no longer has two ways into `done`, one of them mislabelled. **`attention_reason` is deliberately not unified** — `dispatch_infra_recheck` selects exactly `stagnation_pipeline` and re-arms the row, so `pipeline_watch_expired`, `stagnation_discussions` and `review_limit_reached` must stay distinguishable, and each keeps its own GitLab comment; a new test pins that only `stagnation_pipeline` is ever re-armed. The four comments gain one shared closing sentence (`abandon_reassigned`, fr + en) saying the ticket was handed back, appended only when it actually was. `give_up_reviewing` (`review_failures_exhausted`) keeps its own `review_giveup` event: it is the one give-up that starts from `reviewing`, and it already emitted a transition and already reassigned.
- **`dispatch_done_unassigned` skips rows flagged `needs_attention` (Autodev #60, item 2).** A direct consequence of the uniform reassignment above: an abandoned row is `done` and, now, no longer assigned to autodev, so it entered the `post_completion` population. `post_completion` is a *delivery* hook — that is the whole argument behind #52 routing an interrupted row to `closed` — and deploying an abandoned MR is not delivery. Two give-up reasons (`review_limit_reached`, `review_failures_exhausted`) already reassigned and so already slipped through this way; the guard closes it for all five. No nominal completion path raises `needs_attention` and every give-up path does, so the flag separates the two populations exactly. No production impact today: no configured project sets `post_completion`.

### Fixed

- **A blank `review_skill` is now rejected whatever else the project configures (Autodev #74).** The rule "a present-and-blank optional string is a typo, not an absence" was enforced from inside `ProjectValidator.validate_labels!`, which answers a different question — is the label workflow complete — and returns early when the project overrides no label at all. So `review_skill: '  '` on a project that inherits the global labels skipped the check entirely and read as "no skill declared", silently taking the `mr-review` fallback the setting exists to override. `validate_optional_string_fields!` is now called from `validate!` as a sibling of `validate_labels!`, so it holds on its own. `review_skill` is deliberately *not* added to `validate_labels!`'s early-return gate instead: it is not a label, and a project declaring only a review skill would then be rejected for an incomplete label workflow it never claimed to have — a new test pins exactly that config as valid. No runtime impact: `Reviewer#launch_review` reads the setting through `.presence`, so a blank value already behaved as absent; what was missing is the config-time error that names the typo.
- **`NullLogger` in `test/review_publisher_test.rb` is nested in its test class, like every other one in the suite (Autodev #74).** It was the suite's only top-level definition of that name. Nothing collided with it today, but at the top level a second file declaring `NullLogger` would silently *reopen* this one rather than conflict — so a test could pass on a method its own file never defined, and fail when run alone. That is the failure class Autodev #64 added a guard for; this removes the one place it could still start.

- **An escaping exception no longer parks a review in a state nothing re-enqueues (Autodev #74).** `green_first_review` fires `pipeline_green!` — so the row is in `reviewing` — *before* calling `launch_review`, and `dispatch_pipelines` selects `checking_pipeline` only. On the binary path nothing escaped, because `execute_mr_review` rescues `StandardError`; the skill path had four classes that did. Three are now handled where every sibling path already handles them: `ApiUnavailableError` (any of `ReviewPublisher`'s four GitLab calls) hands the row back to `checking_pipeline` with both counters untouched and is then **re-raised**, so the poll still aborts at `PipelineMonitor#check`'s boundary and `abandon_expired_watch` stays unreached; `RateLimitError` goes to `handle_rate_limit` and `AuthenticationError` to `handle_auth_failure`, as at every other `danger_claude_prompt` call site (`IssueProcessor`, `FailureHandler`, `FixCycle`). Before this a transient GitLab 500 while posting the review left the row in `reviewing` until `DormantAudit` picked it up two hours later, spending one of three `dormant_audit_max` attempts — an outage must not spend a budget (Autodev #71), and this was that doctrine broken through a different budget. `ConfigError` (a declared skill missing from the clone) keeps escaping **on purpose**: rescuing it would write an activity row every poll, which keeps the row out of `DormantAudit`'s active arm forever and restarts the age clock, an unbounded and unsignalled loop; parking in `reviewing` is the better of the two available behaviours and `DormantAudit` still reaches a human with `dormant_exhausted`.
- **An inconclusive review no longer resets the pipeline-watch clock, so the 14-day age bound can still fire (Autodev #74).** `review_done!` transitions into `checking_pipeline` and `Issue#stamp_pipeline_watch!` stamps `checking_pipeline_since = Time.current` on *every* transition into that state. That is right for a row that is moving and wrong for one that left the state and came straight back having published nothing: each `:inconclusive` poll restarted the clock while neither counter moved, so a watch stamped 40 days ago produced no abandon and a clock reset to now — and every poll interval paid a fresh clone plus a full skill review under `mr_review_timeout`. This is the family `abandon_expired_watch` exists for (one production ticket polled 29 773 times, Autodev #53), reintroduced through a third outcome its author could not have enumerated. `PipelineMonitor#check` now reads the clock once per poll (`remember_watch_clock`, after `PollTracker`'s seed) and `resume_watch` writes it back on the two outcomes that published nothing — `:inconclusive` and the GitLab outage above. Deliberately **not** `poll_inconclusive!`, which would stand the bound *down* for the cycle: an `:inconclusive` poll did read a pipeline status, it merely could not publish. Same ruling as `locked` in Autodev #69.
- **A blank `review_skill` reads as absent instead of taking the skill path with an empty name (Autodev #74).** `''` is truthy in Ruby, so a YAML-only project spelling the key with an empty value cloned the repo and ran `danger-claude` on a prompt naming no skill at all. `launch_review` now reads `@project_config['review_skill'].presence`, and `bin/autodev`'s boot warning applies the same rule.
- **The review clone is removed (Autodev #74).** `SkillReviewer#review_with_skill` had no `ensure`, so one shallow clone per reviewed ticket accumulated in `/tmp` until reboot. It now carries the same line both sibling clone paths do (`FailureHandler#clone_and_fix`, `FixCycle#execute_fix_cycle`), and it has to be an `ensure` because two outcomes leave by exception: `ApiUnavailableError` from the publish and `ConfigError` from a missing declared skill.
- **`mr.diff_refs` is read through `GitlabHelpers.field` (Autodev #74).** `Gitlab::ObjectifiedHash#method_missing` calls `super` for an absent key, so a merge-request response that simply has no `diff_refs` yet raised `NoMethodError` instead of yielding nil — landing in `launch_review`'s rescue tree rather than answering `:inconclusive`, which is the case the method exists to report.
- **A summary finding with a file and no line no longer renders `(app/x.rb:)` (Autodev #74).** A dangling colon over the field that is missing; `ReviewPublisher` now renders the file alone when there is no line.
- **`bin/autodev`'s "mr-review not installed" warning only fires when a project actually relies on the binary (Autodev #74, spec §7).** A fleet entirely on the skill path was told at every boot that its reviews would be skipped; they are not — they run the project's own skill through `danger-claude`, a hard dependency checked two lines above. The message also goes through `Locales.t` in `fr` and `en` now, like every other boot diagnostic.
- **The prompt names the convention skills, not every skill in the repo (Autodev #74).** `SkillsInjector.skills_instruction` named `all_skills` in full — every `*/SKILL.md` `existing_skills` finds by globbing the cloned repo, not just the four autodev injects. On powerpanne/core that line named 23 skills, including `hotfix` and `resolve-ticket`; on ff/fast/core, 19, including `ship-hotfix-to-production` and `ship-mep-to-production` — asking Claude to load a ship-to-production skill before implementing a ticket. Harmless so far (those workflow skills trigger on their own intent, and the implementation prompt carries none of it), but the review step introduced by the rest of this change now names its own skill explicitly, and a review prompt cannot afford that noise. The distinction: a **convention** skill (`*-conventions`, `*-patterns`) describes how to write code in this project and belongs in a prompt; a **workflow** skill (`mr-review`, `hotfix`, `ship-mep-to-production`) drives a process with its own trigger and its own writes, and is named explicitly by the step that wants it, never broadcast. `skills_instruction` now filters `all_skills` down to the convention/pattern suffixes before building the instruction line. `SkillsInjector.inject`'s return value and `existing_skills`'s glob are unchanged — other callers still depend on `:all_skills` naming the full list.
- **The two source guards stop crediting a form they cannot see, and state what their declarations are worth (Autodev #73).** Three ways round them, each found and re-verified by injection rather than by a green run. (1) `test/api_failure_is_not_a_verdict_test.rb` looked for the *word* `raise` anywhere in a `rescue` clause, so `raise ImplementationError, e.message` counted as a re-raise. It is not one: that class is not an `ApiUnavailableError`, so it never reaches the poll boundary — it falls into the `rescue StandardError` of the fix boundary next door, which marks the ticket `error` and posts a comment blaming the correction, exactly the outcome Autodev #67 removed. A clause now only gets credit for bare `raise` or `raise <the captured variable>`, and a clause raising another class is reported as its own kind (`(re-raises a different class)`) because the fix differs — a conversion the boundary recognises, like `GitlabHelpers.answer`'s, stays declarable rather than banned. (2) The i18n guard (`test/i18n_derived_keys_test.rb`) read interpolated keys only for five hard-coded prefixes — and the keys this product interpolates most, the notification keys, carry no prefix at all, so `:"stagnation_#{type}"` was invisible; it now reads any stem, with the two interpolated symbols that are *not* locale keys declared, the way `NOT_LOCALE_KEYS` already declares its one. A key handed straight to `Locales.t` was checked by nothing — not by a family, not by the literal scan — and four were live (`question_answered_{header,footer}`, `spec_unclear_{header,footer}`); `Locales.t` is now scanned like the wrappers, with its six variable-key call sites declared. And a `DYNAMIC_KEY_SITES` entry that no longer matches any call site is now reported as stale, instead of sitting there excusing nothing while reading like a live statement. (3) **The limit both guards share is now written in both files**: what they check mechanically is that an exception is *declared*; the reason attached to it is an English sentence nothing verifies. That is not hypothetical — two `ALLOWED_SWALLOWS` entries claimed "no read inside" while an unprotected `client.issue` sat underneath them, and the guard was green through a review. A green run means every swallow and every derived-key shape is declared, never that a declaration is true. The two forms still out of reach are named rather than implied: a `rescue Exception` (theoretical — RuboCop's `Lint/RescueException` refuses it) and a file-level `Style/RescueModifier` disable block, which the scanner still catches by the modifier itself.
- **"A transient MR state" has one definition again, and the three readers that disagreed with it are fixed (Autodev #72).** Autodev #69 put the allow-list in one place — `MrStateChecker::TRANSIENT_MR_STATES` — precisely so that widening the door would be a decision and not an accident. That place was a module of `PipelineMonitor`, so the other three readers of `mr.state` could not use it and each answered the question again, differently. Autodev #67, merged after #69, wrote `when 'locked' then :wait` by hand in `PollRouter::ResumeHandler#reenter_destination`: a second copy free to diverge, the shape #62 removed for `fetch_unresolved_discussions`, and adding a state to the list would not have reached the reentry path. `InfraRecheck#mr_open?` tested `== 'opened'`, so a `locked` MR read as "not open" and **spent one of the `infra_recheck_max` attempts** on an answer that meant "wait" — the same defect as burning the budget on an unreachable endpoint, which the rescue three lines below exists to stop. `PollDispatcher#mr_closed_or_merged?` tested `%w[merged closed]`, so a `locked` MR counted as neither closed nor merged — the opposite of the pipeline watch's sort — and let the `post_completion` hook (a deploy) through *while GitLab was performing the merge*; latent, because no project configures the hook today. The list and the predicate now live in `MrState` (`lib/autodev/mr_state.rb`), read by all four. What is shared is the answer to "is this a verdict", **not** the decision that follows: the four ask different questions — was this delivered, does this need reimplementing, does this need re-arming, does this need deploying — and each keeps its own answer. The population is four, checked rather than assumed: the other `state` readers read an *issue*'s state, and `DeployReviewSearch#related_open_iids` filters candidates for a picker rather than taking a verdict, which is written down where the list lives.
- **A GitLab outage no longer spends the stagnation budget (Autodev #71).** `FailureHandler#check_stagnation_and_fix` wrote the stagnation signature *before* calling `clone_and_fix`, so the counter advanced on cycles that attempted no correction at all. Since Autodev #67 the prompt-context read raises from underneath that call (`dispatch_fix` → `fetch_fix_context` → `GitlabHelpers.fetch_full_context`, whose `client.issue` read is the one that goes dark), which means a **selective** GitLab failure — the issue endpoint erroring while the merge-request and pipeline-jobs endpoints answer normally — advanced the counter once per poll and gave the ticket up after `stagnation_threshold` cycles: `done`, `needs_attention: stagnation_pipeline`, `label_attention`, the ticket handed back to its author and a public comment announcing a pipeline stagnation that had not happened (~30 min at the default 5 cycles × 300 s). The signature is now written *after* the attempt returns, which is the ordering the discussions side has always had (`discussion_stagnated?` is called from `MrFixer#finalize_success`, after the push). The invariant is structural rather than a list of causes to remember: every way out of the attempt that is not a completed one is an exception, and the write is the next statement — so the quota deferral that already returned early, the `ApiUnavailableError` that travels to `PipelineMonitor#check`, the `RateLimitError` and the fix crash that park the row in `error` all leave the counter untouched, as does the one non-exception with the same meaning, a poll flagged `poll_inconclusive!` because its danger-claude evaluation never ran (one flag, two bounds — this and the age bound). The threshold arithmetic is unchanged: the check still precedes the write, so a real stagnation is still given up on the same cycle, and "the failure is not code-related" is still a verdict that counts.
- **A GitLab error while assembling a prompt is no longer charged to the correction (Autodev #67).** The gap Autodev #62 declared and left open. `GitlabHelpers.fetch_full_context` builds the danger-claude prompt from the ticket — title, description, comments, and the MR's review history — and its `fetch_issue_context` did a bare `client.issue(...)`. Both fix trees call it from *inside* their `rescue StandardError`: `PipelineFixer#build_fix_context_hash` under `attempt_fix`, `MrFixer::FixCycle#prepare_fix_environment` under `execute_fix_cycle`. So a 500 on the ticket endpoint, between reading the failed jobs and reading the ticket, produced `safe_mark_failed!` → `error`, a public comment saying the pipeline fix or the MR fix had failed, and a spent retry — for something the correction had no part in, and one call deeper than the hoist #62 added precisely to prevent it. The read now goes through `GitlabHelpers.answer(:issue)` and raises `ApiUnavailableError`, both fix boundaries re-raise it above their two handlers, and it ends at the poll's own boundary (`PipelineMonitor#check`, `MrFixer#fix`) with the row exactly as the previous cycle left it — `dispatch_pipelines` / `dispatch_discussions` re-enqueue it next cycle. Two placement details carry the outcome: `dispatch_fix` performs the read **before** `pipeline_failed_code!`, because parking the row in `fixing_pipeline` would be no better than `error` (no dispatch pass selects that state — only the dormant audit, two hours later), and `prepare_fix_environment` reads before injecting skills into a clone it is about to delete. The two other substitutes in the same subtree went with it: `fetch_mr_discussions_context` answered an outage with `''` — an MR nobody has commented on, which is a plausible and wrong thing to tell danger-claude about an MR under review — and `IssueFormatter.append_comments` was "non-fatal: proceed without comments", on the very endpoint bug #32 is about, where the whole instruction lives in a comment posted after the first delivery. `fetch_full_context` is now all-or-nothing. The one swallow left underneath is `append_links`, deliberately: that rescue answers a *capability* gap (the endpoint is absent on older GitLab, hence its `NoMethodError` clause) and its substitute drops a list of sibling ticket titles, deciding nothing.
- **An unreadable MR state no longer re-implements a ticket from scratch, and a `locked` MR waits instead (Autodev #67).** `PollRouter::ResumeHandler#reenter_destination` — the decision taken when a human reposes the todo label on a finished ticket — answered a GitLab error with `:reimplementation`, the most expensive branch autodev has: full clone, danger-claude, push, on a ticket whose MR may already be merged. Same shape as the four readers #62 unpicked, and invisible for the same reason: `:reimplementation` is a perfectly plausible destination. The read now raises and `PollRouter#route` is the boundary — per *issue*, not per pass, because raised any higher it lands in `PollDispatcher#dispatch`'s own `rescue StandardError` and one unreadable MR takes the whole project's cycle down with it, pipeline checks and retries included. Two more decisions on the same method: `locked`, GitLab's transient state while a merge is in flight, moves out of the `else` into a wait — nothing concluded, todo label left where the human put it, re-read next cycle — while `:reimplementation` stays the answer for a state that really is unknown; and `GitlabHelpers.human_comment_since?` stops answering `false` for an outage. `false` there meant "nobody replied", which `open_mr_destination` turns into `:pipeline_check`, the one path that never reads issue comments — so the recette-KO feedback bug #32 exists to catch was silently dropped and the identical MR re-delivered. Its second caller, `PollDispatcher#clarification_received?`, declares its own boundary and keeps answering "not yet": there the row simply stays in `needs_clarification` and the same question is re-asked from GitLab next cycle, so nothing is concluded and nothing acts.
- **The #62 guard's perimeter is now restricted for a stated reason, not by where a diff stopped (Autodev #67).** It covers the code that takes a decision *and acts on it from a read* — the pipeline watch, the MR fix round, and now the reentry decision — and the omissions were checked one by one rather than assumed, each written down in the file: writes (`IssueNotifier`, `LabelManager`, `ActivityLogger`, `ScreenshotUploader`, `ExternalState#notify_stop`), the poll passes whose rescue already sits at the unit-of-work boundary with a substitute meaning "do not act on this row this cycle" (`check_external_state`, `check_post_completion_needed`, `DormantAudit#audit`, `LabelHandover#events`), `IssueProcessor` (no pass re-enqueues an active row, so `error` + backoff is the re-arming mechanism), and `DiscussionSnapshot` (instrumentation, swallows by design). Widening it to those would turn the list into a catalogue of writes and drown the four entries that matter.
- **The #62 source guard sees the inline `rescue`, and no longer mistakes a log message for a re-raise (Autodev #67).** Two blind spots, both proven by injection rather than inferred: with a `fetch_failed_jobs(pipeline) rescue []` and a clause whose only "raise" was inside `log_error "could not raise the alarm: …"` written into `lib/autodev/pipeline_monitor/infra_recheck.rb`, the guard stayed **green**. `NAMED` and `CATCH_ALL` were anchored on `^\s*rescue`, so the modifier form — which is `rescue StandardError` spelled with no `rescue` *line* at all — was invisible; and `RERAISE` was applied to the code with comments stripped but **strings** left in, so any message containing the word made a swallow look like a re-raise. The scanner now strips string literals before comments (that order matters: removing comments first can cut a `#` out of the middle of a literal) and matches everything on the code, and it recognises `\S\s+rescue\s+\S` as a whole one-line clause. This was not theoretical: `MrFixer::StagnationChecker` already carries a `JSON.parse(…) rescue {}` with its own `# rubocop:disable Style/RescueModifier` — the precedent *and* the comment to copy, both one directory from the delivery path, which is the imitation route #62 names as the cause of the original defect. The fixed scanner immediately surfaced two live cases nothing had ever looked at: that one, and **two** modifier rescues in `PostCompletion#kill_process`. Both are declared with what they are (a local `JSON.parse` of a column the method itself wrote; `Errno::ESRCH` / `Errno::ECHILD` on a process group that already died) — declared rather than deleted, because the hazard is the form, not the instance. A second assertion states the modifier rule without a regex and is **complete rather than heuristic**: `Style/RescueModifier` is enabled for this project, so RuboCop guarantees every modifier form in the tree carries the disable directive on its line, and grepping the directive enumerates them exhaustively. Two independent statements of one rule, on purpose.
- **The initial implementation path keeps parking on an unreachable GitLab, on purpose (Autodev #67).** `IssueProcessor#run_implementation` is the third caller of the same helper, and it is the one place #62's answer does not transfer, so the difference is pinned by a test rather than left to look like an oversight. A poll may conclude nothing and re-read next cycle because `dispatch_pipelines` and `dispatch_discussions` re-enqueue their whole population every cycle; an initial implementation has no such pass. The row is mid-flight in an active state, the clone is on disk, `label_doing` is posed and autodev is assigned — nothing would re-enqueue it until `dispatch_dormant_audit` noticed it two hours later. `error` + `next_retry_at` **is** the re-arming mechanism there (`dispatch_retries`), it is bounded and it is reversible, and the comment that path posts is `error_generic`, which names the error class rather than blaming a correction. Since `ApiUnavailableError < AutodevError < StandardError`, `handle_process_error` catches it exactly as it caught the `Gitlab::Error::ResponseError` before: the change is the class of the exception and nothing else. (The read that fails first on this path is in fact `issue_closed?`'s own bare `@client.issue`, one call earlier, which is why the outcome is the same whichever raises.)
- **An i18n key missing from both locales now fails a test, derived from the code instead of listed (Autodev #68).** The project rule is that every user-facing string goes through `Locales.t` / `t_web` **and** exists in `fr` and `en`. `test/locales_test.rb` only ever checked the second half: it compares the two YAML tables to each other, so a key absent from both satisfies parity, and `Locales.t` answers a missing key with the key's own name. That has shipped twice, both found by accident and both fixed in passing by Autodev #66 — `activity_mr_closed` wrote its own name on the GitLab thread of every merged MR, `web_event_abandon` rendered the bare symbol `abandon` in the `/issues/:id` timeline from #60 onwards. Their common shape is why parity can never see it: the key is *derived from a symbol at the call site* — the AASM event name, the symbol handed to `log_activity`, the give-up reason — so nobody writes it and nobody notices it is missing. `test/i18n_derived_keys_test.rb` derives the expected set instead, the way `test/rails_lib_loading_test.rb` derives the lib constant set from the require graph (#64) and `test/api_failure_is_not_a_verdict_test.rb` derives its verdict from a source scan (#62). Nine families, each populated by a runtime enumeration (`Issue.aasm.events`, `ActivityEvent::KINDS` minus the machinery kinds, `HealthReport::CHECKS`, `LabelHandover::EXPECTED_ACTION`, the project's own config columns) or by a scan of the call sites, for the three vocabularies that exist nowhere but in call arguments: the 45 symbols reaching the activity log, the 12 reaching a GitLab notification, and the 7 `attention_reason` values with their **three** sinks each (bare notification key, `activity_<reason>`, `web_errors_explain_attention_<reason>`). Every failure names the key *and* the language *and* the call site that demands it. `dormant_exhausted` is declared as the one reason with no notification key — the dormant audit flags the row and posts nothing on GitLab — rather than filtered out silently. Two guards keep the file derived rather than listed, both modelled on #62's `ALLOWED_SWALLOWS`: a call site handing a *variable* to one of those calls must be declared with the family that covers the values it takes, and an interpolated key namespace (`:"web_foo_#{x}"`) must be declared with the family that enumerates it. A tenth check closes the literal half — the 414 keys spelled out in `lib/`, `app/` and `bin/autodev` must exist too, since a typo in one is just as invisible to parity as an absence.
- **The issue timeline rendered `discussions_snapshot` as a raw token (Autodev #68).** Third occurrence of the defect above, found by the new test on the current tree. `ActivityEvent::KINDS` carries `discussions_snapshot` — the ground-truth instrumentation row `DiscussionSnapshot.capture` persists — and it is not a machinery kind, so `ActivityEvent.user_visible` returns it and both `/issues/:id` and the `/stream` Turbo frame render its kind through `Web::Helpers#event_kind_label`. No `web_event_kind_discussions_snapshot` template existed in either locale, so the Type column showed the bare column value. Added in `fr` and `en`.
- **A merge request GitLab is in the middle of merging is no longer given up (Autodev #69).** GitLab's merge request state machine declares exactly four states — `opened`, `closed`, `merged`, `locked` — and `locked` is the only one that carries no verdict: `MergeRequests::MergeService` wraps the whole merge in `merge_request.in_locked_state`, so the state is entered from `opened` and left either for `merged` or back for `opened`. GitLab's own REST reference puts it plainly: "Searching by `locked` generally returns no results as that state is short-lived and transitional." Since Autodev #66 the sort in `handle_mr_closed` was on "was this delivered", so `locked` fell on the give-up side with everything that is not `merged` — and a poll landing in that window abandoned an MR *in the middle of being delivered*: a public comment saying it had been closed without being merged, which was false, the ticket handed back to its author, `needs_attention: mr_closed_unmerged`, and no end label. Recoverable by hand (reposing the todo label and reassigning autodev re-enters through `PollRouter::ResumeHandler`), but only once somebody has worked out that the comment lies. `locked` now reads like a `running` pipeline instead: the poll leaves without concluding anything and the next cycle decides. The list of states treated that way is an **allow-list** (`MrStateChecker::TRANSIENT_MR_STATES`), so #66's rule is untouched for everything else — a state GitLab adds tomorrow is *unknown*, not transitional, and still goes to the abandon point, because erring towards "a human should look" is recoverable and erring towards "ready for feature review" is not. The wait is bounded: the transient branch falls through to `abandon_expired_watch` instead of returning early from `poll_open_mr`, so an MR wedged in `locked` is given up at `pipeline_watch_max_days` like any other frozen watch, and it deliberately does not raise `poll_inconclusive!`, which would have stood that bound down and made the wait infinite. The window's real duration on our projects is not measurable from the data we keep, so the frequency is unknown; the cost per occurrence was not.
- **A merge request closed without being merged no longer reads as a delivery (Autodev #66).** `poll_open_mr` routes every MR state that is not `opened` to `handle_mr_closed`, which posed the end label unconditionally and left the row unflagged. Merged, that is right — the work is in the target branch. Closed without merging, nothing was delivered, and on powerpanne/core the end label is `Development::Awaiting Feature Review`: a human who rejected the work by closing its MR put the ticket on the PM's board announced as ready for review. The unflagged row was the worse half, because `needs_attention: false` is exactly what `dispatch_done_unassigned` selects on — a rejected MR sat in the `post_completion` population, the one guard Autodev #60 added so a deploy never runs on work autodev did not deliver, and this was the only give-up route that reached it for real. `merged` keeps the delivery ending; every other state (anything GitLab adds later included — `locked` was in that set too until Autodev #69 took it out, see its entry above) is now a give-up through the shared abandon point: `label_attention` instead of the end label — or, unconfigured, no end label at all, which is the behaviour on both real projects today — `needs_attention` with its own `attention_reason` (`mr_closed_unmerged`, deliberately not `stagnation_pipeline`, which `dispatch_infra_recheck` selects and re-arms), the ticket handed back to its author, and a comment saying why. The handback although a human just acted on the MR: assignment is ownership, not notification — the person who closed the MR is not necessarily the ticket's author, and a `done` row is outside `dispatch_unassignment`'s `ACTIVE_STATUSES` sweep and outside the dormant audit's three arms, so nothing else would ever hand it back. No production occurrence yet: the two tickets that reached this route in the 13/08/2026 copy were both merged.
- **The merged-MR activity line rendered its own i18n key (Autodev #66, drive-by).** `handle_mr_closed` logged `:mr_closed` and no `activity_mr_closed` template existed in either locale, so `Locales.t`'s "return the key" fallback wrote `- 07-23 15:14 — activity_mr_closed` onto the GitLab thread — visible on both tickets that reached the path in production. Added in `fr` and `en`, alongside `web_event_abandon`, missing since Autodev #60 gave the give-up paths a shared AASM event and leaving the issue timeline to render the bare symbol `abandon`.
- **A GitLab error no longer delivers a merge request nobody reviewed (Autodev #62).** `PipelineMonitor::ApiHelpers#fetch_unresolved_discussions` answered an API error with `[]`, and `[]` is not "GitLab is down" — it is "this MR has no unresolved thread", which `green_post_review` stamps onto the `no_discussions` guard and turns into `done` + `label_done` + a completion comment + a handback to the author. So a hiccup on the discussions endpoint, at the one moment in a ticket's life when that endpoint decides between delivery and another fix round, shipped an MR carrying open review threads as reviewed: no attention flag, no comment, nothing in the journal. It is the outcome of the 11/08/2026 incident reached by a different route, and it is a standing candidate for the race `DiscussionSnapshot` was added to chase (issue #11859 went `done` with count = 0 while 12 threads were open on the MR). The read now raises `ApiUnavailableError`, the poll ends at `check`'s boundary rescue with the row exactly as the previous cycle left it, and `dispatch_pipelines` re-enqueues it next cycle. It is read before the two side effects in `handle_green` for the same reason: an outage lasting days must not append one "pipeline green" line per poll to the GitLab note.
- **An API failure is no longer a value at all, on any read the pipeline monitor acts on (Autodev #62).** The three paths this ticket names were three instances of one shape — `rescue Gitlab::Error::ResponseError; []` (or `nil`, or `false`) — and the trouble was never the rescue, it was that the substitute is a plausible answer. Fixing them one at a time would have left the shape in place for the next API call added, so the fix is the shape: `GitlabHelpers.answer(:what) { … }` is the single conversion point from "GitLab did not answer" to `ApiUnavailableError`, and there is deliberately no variant that returns a fallback. `fetch_pipeline_jobs` no longer returns nil, `fetch_failed_jobs` is now a filter over it rather than a second read with its own rescue, `dispatch_blocked` has no "jobs unavailable" branch left to forget, and the *default* for a read written with no rescue at all — the natural thing to write — is now the correct one. `test/api_failure_is_not_a_verdict_test.rb` holds the rule as a source-level guard: every place in the pipeline-monitor / MR-fixer tree where a GitLab error is caught and not re-raised is declared in one list with the reason its substitute cannot be mistaken for a verdict (each entry naming the reason its substitute is safe, or — for the one gap that is real and not yet closed — the shape of the gap).
- **The Autodev #62 source guard now sees the form most likely to reintroduce the defect.** Post-review follow-up on the guard itself. `test/api_failure_is_not_a_verdict_test.rb` declared every swallowed GitLab error in the pipeline-monitor / MR-fixer tree, and it worked — it fails on the pre-#62 tree — but it matched a rescue clause only when the clause *named* `Gitlab::Error` or `ApiUnavailableError`. Since `ApiUnavailableError < AutodevError < StandardError`, a plain `rescue StandardError` swallows a failed read just as thoroughly and was invisible to it, which is the more likely regression precisely because it does not look like it has anything to do with GitLab: five such clauses already existed inside the scanned scope. A bare `rescue` and `rescue => e` were invisible for the same reason, and a rescue body whose *comment* merely contained the word "raise" was read as a re-raise. The scanner now matches on whether a clause **can catch** a failed read (adding `StandardError` / `AutodevError` and the catch-all spellings, still ignoring clauses that name an unrelated class such as `RateLimitError`) and strips comments before looking for the re-raise. The five clauses that surfaced are now declared with what each one actually is, which is not the same as saying each one is fine. Three are: `check` (the poll boundary), `execute_mr_review` (an `mr-review` subprocess, no GitLab call) and `extract_diff_hunk` (a local `git diff`). The other two — `attempt_fix` and `execute_fix_cycle` — were first declared as "no read inside", and that was **wrong**: `GitlabHelpers.fetch_full_context` sits underneath both, its `fetch_issue_context` does a bare `client.issue(...)` with no rescue, so a GitLab failure while assembling the prompt context is caught there and imputed to the fix — `error`, a comment blaming the correction, a retry spent. That is the harm the #62 hoist prevents one call shallower, and the honest declaration now names the gap instead of denying it; closing it is the same hoist on a path #62 did not cover, so it gets its own ticket rather than a quiet edit here. Verified both ways: removing a declaration makes the guard name the method, and injecting a `rescue StandardError → []` into the scanned scope makes it fail.
- **A GitLab outage during an infra recheck no longer reads as "the CI is back" (Autodev #62, constat 2).** `InfraRecheck#current_pipeline_verdict` returns `:recovered` on an empty failed-job list, and `fetch_failed_jobs` returned exactly that on an API error — so a blip re-armed a row that had been given up on an infra failure, sending a possibly-still-red pipeline back into `checking_pipeline`. The read now raises and the error reaches `recheck_infra_recovery`'s own boundary rescue, alongside the MR-fetch error it already handled: no re-entry, and no bounded attempt consumed either, on the same reasoning as `check_stagnation_and_fix` — a cycle that never looked must not burn the budget.
- **A danger-claude evaluation that never ran no longer lets the pipeline age bound fire behind it (Autodev #62, constat 3).** The fourth path of the Autodev #56 family, identified during its implementation and left out of scope then. `interpret_eval_result` returns nil both when Claude says the failure is not code-related — a verdict, and waiting is the answer #53's bound is entitled to time out — and when the evaluation could not be performed at all: crashed, timed out, or answered something unparseable. The second case now raises the per-poll `poll_inconclusive!` flag, so the bound stands down for that cycle. `evaluate_with_claude` and `interpret_eval_result` moved from `FailureHandler` to `Evaluator` in the process: splitting one evaluation across two modules — "did it parse?" in the module named after it, "what does its answer mean?" a file away — is part of how the difference between those two nils stayed invisible.
- **One definition of "the unresolved discussion threads of a merge request" (Autodev #62).** There were two, byte-for-byte identical bar the return shape: `MrFixer`'s and `PipelineMonitor`'s, and only the second was on the delivery path. Fixing that one alone would have left the other free to grow the bug back at the next copy-paste, and the two had already drifted once. Both now include `MrDiscussions`; the shape difference stays at the caller, where `MrFixer` maps `build_discussion` over the shared list. MrFixer's copy did not deliver — it sent the row back to `checking_pipeline` — but on an API error it announced "no unresolved discussions", reset the retrigger counter and spent a round on an MR it had never read; it now leaves the row in `fixing_discussions` for the next cycle, with the note untouched.
- **A test file run on its own no longer fails where the full suite passes (Autodev #64).** `config/initializers/load_autodev_config.rb` had two jobs behind one guard: `require_relative '../../lib/autodev'`, which is the only thing that defines the two dozen top-level `lib/` constants `app/` resolves at runtime (`lib/` is deliberately off the Zeitwerk autoload path), and `Web.config = Config.load({})`, which reads the developer's real `~/.autodev/config.yml`. `AUTODEV_SKIP_LEGACY=1` — set by `test/rails_helper.rb` for the second reason, the only one that is a genuine test hazard — skipped both, so under the test harness those constants existed in the process only because some *other* test file had already required them. The full suite passed; the same file run alone raised `NameError`, degraded a probe to `:error`, or 500'd a controller. The worst possible failure mode for a test harness: invisible in integration, visible exactly when iterating on one file. `test/rails_helper.rb` had accumulated nine ad-hoc `require`s papering over it, five of them repeating the same explanation — `Locales` (Phlex sidebar), `GitlabHelpers` (alpha.34), `ScreenshotUploader`, `Redactor` (#59), `ConfigValidator` + `NumericSettings` + the error classes (#58) — each added after a file was caught. Measured before the fix: **40 of 193 test files** depended on that hand-maintained list, and a sixth occurrence was already latent on master (`test/services/autospec/gitlab_importer_test.rb` failed alone on `NameError: uninitialized constant Gitlab`, since `lib/autodev.rb` is also what requires the `gitlab` gem — `config/application.rb` skips `Bundler.require`). The `require` now sits **above** the guard, unconditionally, in every environment, exactly like the `require 'autodev/migration_status'` at the top of `config/initializers/auto_migrate.rb` (#55); the flag keeps gating the disk read alone. There is no useful "pure utilities" subset to load instead — the constants `app/` reaches for include `PipelineMonitor`, `MrFixer` and `IssueProcessor` — so the honest cut is the whole tree, once, at boot; the cost is the `gitlab` / `pastel` / `phlex` / `sqlite3` requires `lib/autodev.rb` performs, already paid on every run by the Sequel-side `test/test_helper.rb`, which never set the flag. `test/rails_lib_loading_test.rb` is the new guard: the Rails-side counterpart of `module_load_test.rb`, it derives the expected constant set from `lib/autodev.rb`'s own require graph instead of listing it, so a module added there is covered without touching a test. Every test file now passes run individually, including the ones the tickets merged alongside this one added afterwards: they inherit the fix without carrying a `require` of their own, which is the property that mattered. No exact counter is quoted here on purpose — the first attempt at this sentence pinned 197 files and went stale one commit later.
- **`GET /issues/:id.json` redacts what the HTML view of the same row already redacted (Autodev #59, follow-up).** The JSON variant of `IssuesController#show` serves `issue_model.attributes` — every column, `dc_stdout` and `dc_stderr` included — and it was the one renderer #59 left unscrubbed after covering both HTML ones. It is the same session, the same cookie and the same authorisation as the page; only the `Accept` header differs, so there was no argument for the asymmetry. No secret was exposed in practice: #59 put the scrub at `record_output`, the single writer of both buffers, so everything stored since is already clean, and the 131 rows carrying those columns were scanned against both `Redactor` patterns with no match. What was wrong is that two of the four readers assumed the columns were clean while nothing structurally guaranteed it. Scrubbed on the serialised blob rather than column by column, matching `IssueShow#render_raw_card` so the two renderers share one rule instead of two lists that drift — which also covers `error_message`, named in `Redactor`'s own comment as a place the PAT lands (`ShellHelpers.run_cmd` puts the whole `oauth2:<token>@…` command into the `GitError` message). Both substitutions are plain text, so redacting after serialisation cannot produce invalid JSON, and a test pins that the response still parses and still carries every attribute. The CLI `--errors` path keeps printing `dc_stderr` verbatim, deliberately: it reads the local database on a machine whose `~/.autodev/config.yml` already holds the PAT in cleartext.
- **The `monitoring:` block joins the numeric registry, closing the hole Autodev #58 left and Autodev #60 fell into.** #58 separated the type question from the range question so that a typo in a numeric setting would be *reported* instead of coerced, and it covered the flat top-level globals and the per-project columns. It did not cover the five numeric settings nested under `monitoring:` — and two of those shipped in the same release as part of #60's mr-review health check, read as `(value || DEFAULT).to_i`. `.to_i` on a non-number is `0`, and `0` is meaningful for all five, so a typo did not fail: it reconfigured the setting in silence, which is precisely the failure mode #58 exists to end. `review_failure_window_seconds: "6h"` read as a 0-second window, so the rolling query asked for events newer than "now", counted none, and the check answered `:ok` for ever — the alert built so the 2026-08-11 outage would surface in hours rather than weeks was off, with a green `/healthz` to prove it. `review_failure_threshold: "trois"` had the mirror failure: `count >= 0` is always true, so the check warned permanently. The other three (`poll_stale_factor`, `stuck_active_after_seconds`, `activity_event_retention_seconds`) were saved only by their `max(configured, derived)` floor semantics, which is luck, not design. The fix is a registry for the block rather than two patched readers, so the sixth setting added under `monitoring:` does not have to remember: `NumericSettings::MONITORING_SPECS` declares all five, `ConfigValidator` refuses to boot on a bad one (naming it `monitoring.<field>`, since the field names are only unique inside the block), and every reader goes through `NumericSettings.monitoring_integer`, which falls back to the documented default — protective — instead of to `0`. Belt and braces on purpose: `HealthReport` and `ActivityEventJanitor` are also built from hand-made hashes by `bin/rails runner` and by the test suite, neither of which calls `validate_globals!`. A threshold of `1` stays legitimate ("warn on the first failing ticket"); it is `0` that was never a choice anybody made.
- **A boot-time warning can no longer take the supervisor down with it.** `bin/autodev`'s `warn_rejected_numeric_settings` (#58) is the only `warn_*` helper in `bootstrap` that reads the database — the others read the already-parsed config hash and cannot raise — and it had no `rescue`, so a locked or corrupt SQLite file, or a half-written `projects` row, would abort the supervisor before any child was spawned. #55 deliberately made `bin/autodev` refuse to start only on a condition it can *name* (an unapplied migration → `ConfigError` → exit 1); an unhandled exception out of an advisory warning is a second, unnamed refusal with no diagnosis attached. It now logs that it could not audit and carries on, matching what every other best-effort reader and writer on these tables does.
- **A GitLab outage or a Claude quota cut no longer gives a watched ticket up, and the abandon message no longer claims the pipeline stood still (Autodev #56).** alpha.47's age bound on a pipeline watch fires as the last statement of the poll, on the condition that the poll left the row in `checking_pipeline` — deliberately a condition rather than a list of the branches that go nowhere. Its design spec asserted that a GitLab outage could therefore never trigger it ("`check` raises before reaching the call"). Autodev #51, developed in parallel and merged just before, made that false: `fetch_pipeline_jobs` rescues the API error *inside* itself and returns `nil`, so `dispatch_blocked` logs "jobs unavailable, rechecking next poll" and returns **normally** — a 14-day-old ticket was abandoned over a transient 500. The red path had the same shape already (`fetch_failed_jobs` answers an error with `[]`, read as `handle_no_failed_jobs` → "staying in checking_pipeline"), and the two Claude-quota deferrals are a third: there the row is left in place because autodev could not act, so a pipeline that turned green on day 15 during an outage was given up with a comment saying it had not moved for a fortnight. Abandoning is not cheap and not recoverable — terminal status, `needs_attention`, `label_done` on GitLab, a public comment, and `pipeline_watch_expired` is excluded from the infra-recheck pass on purpose, so nothing re-arms the row. A poll that cannot conclude now raises a per-poll flag (`poll_inconclusive!`, cleared at the top of every `check`) and the bound stands down for that cycle, with one log line saying why. Only a poll that actually read a pipeline status can expire a watch. The three user-facing strings were reworded in both locales accordingly: the give-up says the watch ran for more than N days *without ever reaching a verdict*, which is what actually happened — the previous wording was also wrong for an infra failure recurring with a fresh signature every day, a pipeline that moved plenty and still never transitioned.
- **`activity_events` has a retention window, so the table stops growing forever (Autodev #57).** Autodev #53 collapsed the per-poll `danger_claude` row that made 53 % of the table and shipped a one-shot rake purge for the arrears — but nothing ever deleted a row on a schedule, and the same release opened a slower leak in the same family with Autodev #50's `heartbeat` kind. Measured on the 12/08/2026 production copy: 930 816 rows, 275 MB of file, 187 MB of it `activity_events` and its indexes. What #53's collapse leaves behind is *almost entirely machinery* — that day production wrote 720 `poller` rows, 720 `usage` rows and ~650 `heartbeat` rows against ~50 `danger_claude` and ~20 `transition`, i.e. 97 % of the remaining growth is rows nobody asked for. New `Autodev::ActivityEventJanitor` deletes the rows whose `kind` is in the new `ActivityEvent::MACHINERY_KINDS` (`poller`, `error`, `usage`, `heartbeat`) past a retention window, run daily by `PruneActivityEventsJob` (`config/recurring.yml`, production only, 04:45) — **recurring on purpose**: a second manual rake task is what #53 already shipped and it cannot bound growth. The business kinds (`transition`, `danger_claude`, `discussions_snapshot`) are never touched: they are the audit trail the issue timeline renders, and #53's collapse already bounds them per issue. First production pass drops ~43 200 rows; steady state is ~4 300 machinery rows instead of an unbounded count. It does **not** `VACUUM` — reclaiming the arrears' 187 MB stays #53's deliberate manual procedure.
- **The retention window is derived from `stuck_active_after`, not configured next to it (Autodev #57).** `Issue.without_activity_since` deliberately counts heartbeats — that is the entire mechanism by which a live-but-quiet worker stays out of `dispatch_dormant_audit`'s population (Autodev #50). So a purge that reaches inside `HealthReport#stuck_active_after` does not merely lose a log line: it deletes the row that proves the worker alive, and the audit — which mutates by `update_all`, outside the `limits_concurrency` that serialises `IssueProcessJob` — hands a ticket somebody is working on back to the dispatcher. The window is therefore `12 × HealthReport#stuck_active_after`, i.e. **24 h at default settings**, and it widens automatically when a project raises `dc_timeout` / `post_completion_timeout` / `mr_review_timeout`, exactly like the window it is derived from. `monitoring.activity_event_retention_seconds` is a **floor, never a ceiling** (same idiom as `monitoring.stuck_active_after_seconds`): you may keep machinery rows longer for forensics, you may not keep them for less time than the safety window. Both machinery readers keep reading — `HealthReport#check_poller` wants the newest `poller` row and its staleness window (`max(poll_interval × 3, 900 s)`) sits two orders of magnitude inside the retention; `UsageGate.state` wants the newest `usage` row, trusted for two poll intervals.
- **One definition of "a row somebody asked to see" (Autodev #57).** `ActivityEvent.user_visible` hid `heartbeat` alone while `Web::Helpers#weekly_activity_counts` carried its own hardcoded `('poller','error','heartbeat')` list — two definitions of the same notion, free to diverge at the next kind added. They already had: the per-cycle Claude-quota verdict (`kind: 'usage'`, Autodev #46) fell through the gap and was counted as work by the "Activité de la semaine" sparkline. That was a 3 % distortion when `danger_claude` ran at 23 800 rows/day; once #53 dropped it to ~50/day it became the *majority* of the bar (382 `usage` against 30 `danger_claude` on 12/08/2026). The scope now excludes every machinery kind and the sparkline goes through it, so the set that is invisible is exactly the set that is purgeable — a row nobody renders and whose only reader wants just the newest one. `Issue.without_activity_since` still does **not** apply the scope, which remains the whole point of #50. New tests: `services/autodev/activity_event_janitor_test.rb` (14, including the #50 pair — a purge must not turn a live-but-quiet worker dormant, and must not hide a genuinely dormant row either — plus the "purged kinds are exactly the ones `user_visible` hides" pin), `jobs/prune_activity_events_job_test.rb`, and additions to `activity_event_test.rb` + `weekly_activity_counts_test.rb`.
- **A numeric per-project setting is now bounded, and a rejected value is visible (Autodev #58).** `Config::INTEGER_FIELDS` answered two different questions with one list — "coerce this to an Integer" and "reject it unless it is > 0" — and the conflation left both of the settings that drive a safety net unguarded. `mr_review_timeout` (#54) had a floor but no ceiling, and it is a term of `HealthReport#longest_worker_timeout`, so `86400000` instead of `86400` in one project's config widened `stuck_active_after` from 12 hours to ~5.5 years and switched off *both* mechanisms that catch a dead worker — the dormant audit and the "Issues bloquées" card — with nothing to show for it: no error, a green dashboard, and no frozen row ever detected again. `pipeline_watch_max_days` (#53) had the opposite problem: it was deliberately kept *out* of that list because `0` is meaningful there (it disables the age bound), which left it with no coercion and no validation at all, so a non-numeric value read as `.to_i` → `0` → last-resort bound disabled, also in silence. A new `NumericSettings` registry separates the type question from the range question: a field declares `[min, max]` in one line, `min: 0` says "0 is a sentinel" without also saying "anything that coerces to 0 is fine", and `NumericSettings.integer` never turns garbage into `0`. Every layer reads the same declaration — `ConfigValidator` (globals), `ProjectValidator` (a YAML `projects:` entry), the `Project` model's two-step validation (`numericality` for the type, then the range), and therefore the dashboard's per-project config form, whose number inputs now carry the declared `min`/`max`, whose hints state the accepted range, and which reports a bad entry instead of silently clearing the field to the global default (a non-numeric entry used to become `nil`, i.e. "unset"). The three worker timeouts are bounded to 60 s…6 h — 6× the largest baked default, 8× the longest successful mr-review on record, and enough to cap the dormant-detection window at 12 h — and `pipeline_watch_max_days` to 0…365 days. Finally, `bin/autodev` warns at boot, in the configured locale, naming the project, the field, the value and the range, for a value already sitting in the `projects` table: a bad *file* aborts the supervisor, but a bad *row* must not, or there would be no dashboard left to fix it from.
- **The captured subprocess output is scrubbed before it is stored, not only before it is logged (Autodev #59).** `give_up_reviewing` passed the mr-review failure *message* through `Redactor` — because the logger on that path in production is `Autodev::JobLogger`, which does not scrub, and mr-review holds the same GitLab PAT autodev does — and then wrote `dc_stdout` / `dc_stderr` onto the row **raw**. The audit that asymmetry called for found it was not one site but **fourteen**: every `dc_stdout:` / `dc_stderr:` write in the product persisted unredacted output, and had since the columns were introduced — 3 in each of the three `ErrorHandler`s, plus `IssueProcessor#persist_finalize`, `QuestionHandler#finalize_question`, `MrFixer::FixCycle#finalize_success`, `PipelineFixer#complete_fix_round` and `Reviewer#give_up_reviewing`. Two of those are **success** paths, so this was never confined to failures either; the review give-up was simply the first one whose sibling log line scrubbed, which is what made it visible. The scrub now sits in `ProcessRunner#record_output`, the **single writer** of the two buffers, so one line covers all fourteen sites and any future one; the streams handed *back* to the caller stay byte-identical, because `capture_session_and_text` JSON-parses stdout and the rate-limit / auth detectors match signatures in it. Contrary to the report's premise the exposure was never latent: `IssuesController#show` hands the view `issue_model.attributes` and `IssueShow#render_raw_card` renders `JSON.pretty_generate` of all of them, so both columns were already on the dashboard — raw, for every signed-in viewer — and on `GET /issues/:id.json`. Scanned the 131 production rows carrying output against both `Redactor` patterns: **no hit**, so no backfill migration; rows written before this change are covered at render instead.
- **`/healthz` no longer walks the migration directories on every request (Autodev #60, item 3).** `HealthReport#check_migrations` (added by Autodev #55) calls `Autodev::MigrationStatus.pending`, which is a set difference between two halves: the versions parsed from the files under `db/migrate` + `db/queue_migrate`, and the versions recorded in `schema_migrations`. Only the second half can change while the process runs. The first was re-walked from the filesystem on every call — two directory traversals per hit on the one endpoint designed to be polled by an external probe. The file half is now memoised per process, keyed on the resolved migration paths and guarded by a mutex (the call happens inside a Puma request thread). `schema_migrations` is still re-read every time, so a migration applied by hand while autodev runs is still seen: a cached *verdict* would have been the real bug.
- **`PollRouter#reenterable?` no longer claims its API cost is transient (Autodev #60, item 4).** Documentation only, no behaviour change. The gate spends one `issue_label_events` call per cycle on every row that is `closed` in the DB while its GitLab issue is still open, assigned to autodev and todo-labelled. The comment said that population "empties itself"; it does not, for exactly the case the gate exists to reject — a ticket closed from the dashboard with the todo label left in place has a label event that predates `finished_at` forever, so the question is re-asked, and answered no, every poll interval indefinitely. Measured on the 12/08/2026 production copy: 60 `closed` rows, none of them still open + todo-labelled on GitLab, i.e. **zero recurring calls today**. Hence the cost is now stated honestly in the code rather than bounded — a cache would buy complexity for an empty set. Re-measure before adding one.
- **A queued job no longer replays the side effects of a transition that did not happen (Autodev #61).** The queue is not a snapshot: `dispatch_pipelines` enqueues the whole `checking_pipeline` population every cycle, so any job outliving the poll interval leaves duplicates behind it — and once alpha.47's `manual`-pipeline resolution turned that job from a millisecond no-op into a full mr-review run, that became the normal case. The state machine did not stop the duplicates. `whiny_transitions: false` makes an impossible transition a silent no-op rather than a raise, and the callers treat the event as a command that always succeeds: `green_first_review` calls `launch_review` after a `pipeline_green!` that did nothing, and `give_up_reviewing` re-applies `label_done`, reassigns the author and posts a GitLab comment after a `review_giveup!` that did nothing. In production on 11/08/2026 that put **486 comments on 28 powerpanne tickets in two hours** — issue #15839 alone took 26 identical "mr-review failed 5 times" comments, one every 105 seconds, every one of them after its last real transition, while its `review_failure_count` climbed to 30 for 5 real rounds. `IssueProcessJob#perform` now checks the row against `DISPATCHED_FROM`, the state each action's dispatch pass selects on, and logs a skip instead of running. One declaration covers all seven actions; the row was being read anyway, so it costs no query. This is not a substitute for `limits_concurrency` — that answers "is another job touching this row", this answers "does the work this job was queued for still need doing".
- **The review step is named after the step, not after one of its two paths (Autodev #83).** Documentation and two locale strings only, no behaviour change. Since Autodev #74 the review step has two paths — the `mr-review` binary and the project's declared `review_skill` — but eight places written when there was only one still used "mr-review" as the name of the step itself, so a reader concluded the binary always runs: three in `docs/powerpanne-lifecycle.md` (the lifecycle list, the automatic-review sentence, the mermaid label), two in `docs/usage/autodev-technical-usage.md` (the state-diagram label, the "green → mr-review → delivery" edge-case row), three in `CLAUDE.md` (the same diagram, the same row, the health-probe row). One occurrence was more than a shorthand, and the only one a user ever reads: `web_project_edit_desc_mr_review_timeout` (fr + en), rendered in the project configuration form, said the setting bounds "a single mr-review run" — `SkillReviewer` bounds a skill review with it too, deliberately, because the duration profile is the same, so the description was factually false for every project on the skill path. Two further shorthands the ticket's inventory missed are corrected with them, both in `docs/usage/autodev-technical-usage.md`: the settings-table row for `mr_review_timeout` (the same false claim as the locale) and the usage-gate sentence about `review_count == 0`. **Nothing is renamed**: the `mr_review_timeout` setting, the `mr_review` health check and the `web_admin_health_check_mr_review` key all keep their names — each one covers both paths, renaming reaches the migration, the rows already in the database, the project YAMLs and both locales for a cosmetic gain, and the health check's rendered label ("Revue automatique" / "Automated review") is already path-neutral.
- **What autodev *says* when a review fails no longer names the binary that may not have run it (Autodev #83).** Strings only, no behaviour change. `finalize_review_failure` and `give_up_reviewing` are shared by both review paths since Autodev #74, so everything they emit spoke for the skill path too while still calling it "mr-review": the two activity-journal lines (`activity_review_failed`, `activity_review_failures_exhausted`, rendered on the issue page), the Slack notification `review_failures_exhausted`, and the two log lines on the same methods.
